Doctoral Dissertation

mass spectra in ﬁnal states with
low-momentum leptons using 140 fb 1 of

Abstract
Although the Standard Model (SM) has attained substantial success in describing parti-
cle physics phenomena, it has confronted major challenges such as its inability to explain
dark matter and to address the hierarchy problem. Hence, the ultimate goal of modern
particle physics is to discover the physics beyond the SM. Supersymmetry (SUSY), a
yet-to-be-found symmetry between fermions and bosons, is one of the leading candidates
of physics beyond the SM. Under the framework of Minimal Supersymmetric Standard
Model (MSSM) with R-parity conservation, scenarios with light higgsinos are viable can-
didates of new physics, as they are consistent with constraints from collider experiments
and dark matter observations. Experiments at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) have
the potential to search for such scenarios, however, the sensitivities are limited by exper-
imental diculties owing to the compressed mass spectra of the higgsinos.
The dissertation presents the search for higgsinos using 140 fb 1 of proton-proton
collisions at center-of-mass energy of ps =1 3T e Va tt h eL H C ,t a r g e t i n gﬁ n a ls t a t e s
with low-momentum leptons. The sensitivity to higgsinos in the target mass regions has
been considerably improved by the development and application of low- pT lepton track
identiﬁcation algorithms and the introduction of neural-network-based event selection
techniques. No signiﬁcant excess in data has been observed in the unblinded dataset,
and exclusion limits have been set on the target higgsino scenarios. In the context of the
simpliﬁed higgsino model with  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1)=2  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1), higgsino mass of up to 130
GeV has been excluded for  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) = 1–2 GeV. This mass splitting region has been
covered for the ﬁrst time at the LHC, extending the limit by about 40 GeV in higgsino
mass, where the original limit was set by the LEP2 experiment.

Preface
The dissertation covers my works on search for higgsinos in the ATLAS experiment [ 1]
during my PhD program at The University of Tokyo in 2019–2024. The leading channel
of pure higgsino searches in ATLAS have been carried out by requiring a high- pT hadronic
jet and targeting signatures with high missing momentum ( Emiss
T )f r o mh i g g s i n o sa n dt w o
low-pT leptons ( e+e,µ +µ)o r i g i n a t i n gf r o mt h eh i g g s i n od e c a y ˜0
2 ! ˜0
1Z⇤.G i v e n t h a t
higgsino mass splitting  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) is typically of a few GeV, the sensitivity had been
largely limited due to diculties in signal event tagging. This research overcomes these
diculties by developing and exploiting new low- pT lepton identiﬁcation algorithms with
dedicated optimizations for leptons in low- pT regimes, extending the search sensitivity to
higgsinos with compressed mass spectra. The research uses the full “LHC Run 2” dataset
taken in 2015–2018, corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 140 fb 1.
In 2019–2020, as a preparatory work to search for new physics in the ATLAS ex-
periment, I have conducted phenomenological studies by parameter scans and proposed
that a supersymmetric model having higgsinos with compressed mass spectra is one of
the most motivated candidates of new physics under the current experimental and the-
oretical constraints. Targeting higgsino searches in ﬁnal states with low- pT leptons, I
started R&D studies on the identiﬁcation algorithm of low- pT electrons in 2021. As the
studies gradually converged in 2022, I shifted my focus onto the search analysis for higgsi-
nos with compressed mass spectra. The following are brief descriptions of my signiﬁcant
contributions to these works.
Development of the low- pT electron identiﬁcation algorithm
The new low- pT electron identiﬁcation algorithm has been designed to identify low-
pT electrons primarily based on the track information given by the ATLAS inner
detectors, with less emphasis on energy clusters in the calorimeters, which are not
reconstructed for pT below 2 GeV. Using deep neural network (DNN), the algorithm
exploits the track and calorimeter cluster information in an inclusive manner. I have
conducted all of its development works by myself: studies and selection of input de-
tector variables, design of the DNN structure, determination of the selection criteria
and performance evaluation, and eciency measurement with data. Namely, I have
created the low- pT electron identiﬁcation algorithm from scratch and succeeded in
its implementation to the undermentioned search analysis for higgsinos.
Search for higgsinos with compressed mass spectra

reviews the experimental apparatus: LHC and ATLAS detector. Section 3 describes the

identiﬁcation methods. Sections 6–9 describe the principal methods of the analysis: event

1. Introduction
What are the fundamental constituents of matter and force? The ultimate question has
been pursued throughout the history of human beings. As of today, we deem elementary
particles to be the most fundamental constituents. The nature of these elementary parti-
cles is described to a great extent by the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics [ 2–11].
The SM has succeeded in explaining experimental results with high precision.
Nevertheless, the SM has some major deﬁciencies, both experimentally and theoret-
ically. One leading example is its failure to explain the existence of dark matter (DM),
strongly implied by multiple observation results of the universe [ 12–15]. Anisotropies of
the cosmic microwave background (CMB) and gravitational lensing e↵ects indicate that
the mass content of dark matter in the whole universe should be 5-6 times more than that
of ordinary baryonic matter. Another issue of the SM is its incapability of giving masses
to the neutrinos. Although the SM expects neutrinos to be massless, observations of
neutrino oscillations [ 16, 17]i n d i c a t em a s s i v en e u t r i n o s . O nt h et h e o r e t i c a ls i d e ,t h eS M
incorporates strong, weak, and electromagnetic interactions, however, it does not unify
them as a single force. The theoretical framework for the uniﬁcation of these three forces
is called “Grand Uniﬁed Theory (GUT)” [ 18–20], primarily motivated by the observation
of charge quantization. In addition, the inclusion of gravitational interactions and solu-
tions to the hierarchy problem [ 21]w o u l db ef u r t h e rn e c e s s a r yf o ram o r ef u n d a m e n t a l
theory.
The utmost priority of modern particle physics is to therefore search for new physics
beyond the SM and to provide a clearer picture of microscopic phenomena surrounding
us. Although there are many theories which do so, none of them have been experimentally
conﬁrmed to be correct. Supersymmetry (SUSY) [ 22–28]i so n eo ft h el e a d i n gc a n d i d a t e s
of physics beyond the SM, which has the potential of such impact. SUSY imposes trans-
formation relations between bosons and fermions, predicting new particles with quantum
numbers identical to their SM partners with the exception of spin; SM fermions (bosons)
have bosonic (fermionic) SUSY partners. Theoretical aspects of SUSY was ﬁrst proposed
and established in the 1970s in the context of quantum ﬁeld theory and string theory [ 29,
30]. Although experimental evidence of SUSY has not been found, the theory has been
held in high regard due to its strong prediction power and theoretical robustness [ 31].
SUSY not only gives viable candidates of DM [ 32, 33], but also gives solutions to grand
uniﬁcation and the hierarchy problem [ 34–39]. In light of arguments regarding naturalness
[38, 40], higgsinos, SUSY partners of the Higgs bosons 1,a r ee x p e c t e dt oh a v em a s sn e a r
the electroweak scale [ 41–44].
1In the minimal supersymmetric extension of the SM, an additional Higgs doublet is introduced,
causing the model to have four additional Higgs bosons.

This dissertation presents searches for higgsino production at the Large Hadron Col-
lider (LHC), targeting scenarios where the lightest supersymmetric particles (LSPs) are
assumed to be higgsinos, within the framework of the minimal supersymmetric extension
of the SM (MSSM) [ 45, 46]w i t hR - p a r i t yc o n s e r v a t i o n . I ns u c hs c e n a r i o s ,t h eh i g g s i -
nos are expected to have compressed mass spectra, with typical mass splitting values of
O(0.1–1 GeV). The general strategy of this search is to target signatures with two low-
momentum leptons, which are emitted when the second-lightest neutral higgsino decays
into the lightest neutral higgsino.
1.1 The Standard Model
1.1.1 Overview
The Standard Model (SM) of particle physics is a quantum ﬁeld theory which best de-
scribes three of the four known forces which interact between elementary particles or
ﬁelds: strong, weak, and electromagnetic interactions, with the exception of gravity. The
SM incorporates the three interactions as internal symmetries, formulated as the unitary
gauge group SU (3)C ⇥ SU (2)L ⇥ U (1)Y .
The particle content of the SM is as follows. Table 1.1 shows the fermions in the SM.
The fermions are roughly divided into two categories: quarks which interact with strong
force and leptons which do not. Quarks and leptons both consist of three generations,
hence we introduce a notation using generation numbers i =1 , 2, 3. Up, charm, and top
quarks are denoted as up-type quarks ui = u, c, t,a n dd o w n ,s t r a n g e ,a n db o t t o mq u a r k s
are denoted as down-type quarks di = d, s, b.S i m i l a r l y f o r l e p t o n s , t h e t h r e e n e u t r i n o s

addition, the SU (2)L gauge group engages only with left-handed fermions, hence, the left-
handed components of the fermions constitue SU (2)L doublets, whereas the right-handed
components constitute SU (2)L singlets. In summary, the SM fermions are denoted as

Table 1.2 shows the bosons in the SM. There are four gauge bosons: gluons which
mediate strong force, W and Z bosons which mediate weak force, and photons which me-
diate electromagnetic force. The Higgs boson emerges as a result of the Higgs mechanism
[9–11], where the spontaneous breaking of electroweak SU (2)L ⇥ U (1)Y gauge symmetry
gives mass to W and Z bosons.
The interactions of particles in the SM are characterized by the Lagrangian 3 of the
SM. The Lagrangian formalism is a convenient way to describe quantum ﬁeld theories
with Lorentz invariance and gauge invariances. The SM Lagrangian could be written as

1.1.2 Remaining problems of the Standard Model
Although it has been proven that the SM describes the microscopic phenomena sur-
rounding us with high precision, it is strongly believed that it is merely a low-energy
approximation; the SM cannot be the ultimate theory of everything as it contains ma-
jor experimental and theoretical problems. This section brieﬂy highlights some of those
problems.
Dark matter
Cosmological observations have shown that ordinary baryonic matter does not fully ac-
count for the mass content of the universe; it is expected that there be 5-6 times more
mass content from “invisible” matter, which we refer to as dark matter (DM) .E a r l ye v -
idences of DM come from rotation curves of galaxies [ 48, 49]; the rotation velocities of
galaxies cannot be explained without the assumption of unordinary matter that we do
not observe. Fig. 1.1 displays an example of a rotation curve. The velocity of the galaxy
exhibits a ﬂat behavior at large distances from the galactic center; this implies the exis-
tence of galactic halo, namely DM, in these regions. Further evidences from gravitational
lensing e↵ects [50]a n da n i s o t r o p i e so fC M B[12, 13] also strongly suggest the existence of
DM.
Figure 1.1: Rotation curve of galaxy NGC 6503 [ 51]. The points show the rotation velocities of the galaxy
with respect to the distances from its center. The solid line shows the ﬁtted velocity with the assumption
of DM. The dotted, dashed, and dash-dotted lines each represents the contributions from gas, disk, and
DM (halo).
There are many proposed candidates of DM. Elementary particles beyond the SM, such
as Weakly Interacting Massive Particles (WIMPs) and axions, are leading DM candidates.
WIMP DM candidates are expected to be thermal relics. As the temperature of the
universe dropped below the WIMP mass scale, the number density of WIMPs would
decrease exponentially, eventually to a point where their interactions with surrounding
particles become negligible. This “freeze out” of WIMPs would lead to a substantial
4

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
number of WIMP relics in the universe today. In the case in which the freeze out is
explained by a s-channel annihilation, the relic density of a WIMP is roughly given by

to be ⌦ DMh2 ' 0.12 from recent observations [ 13], if all DM is assumed to be WIMPs,
typical WIMP mass would be O(100–1000) GeV.
Nonthermally produced objects such as axions [ 53–56] and dynamical, non-particle-like
objects such as primordial black holes [ 57–59]a r ea l s oc o n s i d e r e da sD Mc a n d i d a t e s .

weak hypercharge YW . The electric charges of charged leptons Q(l)a n dd o w nq u a r k s
Q(d)i sr e l a t e db y|Q(l)| =3 |Q(d)|. As the weak hypercharges are arbitrary, there is no
reason for this integer multiple relation to hold. Moreover, the sum of weak hypercharges
for all fermion content must be zero for U (1)Y gauge anomaly to cancel, which is indeed
the case for the SM. This quantized nature of charges has led to the idea of Grand Uniﬁed
Theory (GUT), which uniﬁes the strong and electroweak interactions into a single force
described by a larger symmetry group. Therefore, the SM is strongly believed to be
merely a low-energy approximation of GUT.
Another motivation of GUT is the agreement of gauge couplings at high energy. Fol-
lowing the renormalization group equations, the gauge couplings of SU (3)C , SU (2)L,a n d
U (1)Y run to certain values at higher energy scales. The three couplings are expected
to take similar values at a certain grand uniﬁcation scale ⇤ GUT,w h i c ha l s oi n d i c a t e st h e
existence of GUT.
Although it is beyond the scope of modern particle physics experiments, it is worth-
while to mention that the fundamental theory of everything is expected to also accomodate
gravity. However, theories of quantum gravity cannot be constructed straightforwardly,
as gravitational interaction is non-renormalizable. Theories such as loop quantum gravity
[60–64] and string theory have been studied as leading candidates of gravity uniﬁcation
theories.
Hierarchy problem and the ﬁne-tuning of the Higgs mass
The SM cannot explain the large disparity between the energy scale of the weak force
and the gravitational force. This problem is often referred to as the hierarchy problem.
A famous manifestation of the hierarchy problem is the ﬁne-tuning problem of the Higgs
mass. As already shown in Table 1.2, it has been discovered that the mass of the Higgs
boson is 125 GeV, however, this value is unnaturally small compared to the energy scale
of ultraviolet theories. If the SM is a low-energy approximation of a more general theory
5

=== PDF PAGE 17 ===
having an energy scale of ⇤, calculations of the Higgs mass squared will include quadratic
radiative corrections with a magnitude of O(⇤2):
m2
h = m2
h0 + m2
h, m2
h ⇠ ⇤2, (1.9)
where mh0 is the bare mass of the Higgs. If we assume the SM to hold up to the Planck
scale MPl ⇠ 1019 GeV, this implies the cancellation of 34 orders of magnitude between
m2
h and  m2
h. For this to happen in the SM, “ﬁne-tuning” of the parameters is necessary,
which would be quite unnatural. To solve this problem of ﬁne-tuning, it is therefore
required to extend the SM in a way that the quadratic radiative corrections to the Higgs
mass disappear in principle.
1.2 Supersymmetry
Supersymmetry (SUSY) is a symmetry between bosons and fermions. It introduces a
supersymmetric operator Q,w h i c ht r a n s f o r m sb o s o n i cs t a t e st of e r m i o n i cs t a t e sa n dv i c e
versa:
Q|Bosoni= |Fermioni,Q |Fermioni= |Bosoni. (1.10)
In particular, Q and its hermitian conjugate Q† are fermionic operators, or spinors, which
satisfy the following anti-commutation and commutation relations 4:
{Q↵ ,Q †
˙↵ } = 2µ
↵ ˙↵ Pµ (1.11)
{Q↵ ,Q  } =0 , {Q†
˙↵ ,Q †
˙ } =0 , (1.12)
[Q↵ ,P µ]=0 , [Q†
˙↵ ,P µ]=0 . (1.13)
↵ and  are spinor indices, and P µ is the ordinary four-momentum generator of spacetime
translations.
In SUSY, single-particle states belong to the irreducible representations of the supresym-
metric algebra called supermultiplets. Each supermultiplet contains both fermion and
boson states, related to each other by Eq. ( 1.10). These fermion and boson states are
referred to as SUSY partner particles of each other. Since the squared-mass operator
PµP µ commutes with Q and Q†,t h ef e r m i o na n db o s o nb e l o n g i n gt ot h es a m es u p e r -
multiplet have equal mass. In addition, the supersymmetric operators also commute with
operators of gauge transformations; the particles in the same supermultiplet have the
same quantum numbers, including color, weak isospin, and electric charge.
It can be further proved that the number of degrees of freedom for fermions and
bosons in the same supermultiplet are equal [ 28]. Accordingly, two main types of su-
permultiplets partake in supersymmetric extensions of the SM: chiral supermultiplets
and vector supermultiplets. A chiral supermultiplet consists of a two-component Weyl
fermion and a complex scalar ﬁeld. A vector supermultiplet consists of a vector boson and
at w o - c o m p o n e n tW e y lf e r m i o n . C o n s i d e r i n gr e n o r m a l i z a b i l i t ya n dg a u g et r a n s f o r m a t i o n
properties, it is expected that the Weyl fermions in chiral supermultiplets correspond to
the SM fermions, and the vector bosons in vector supermultiplets correspond to the SM
4We assume here that there is only one set of supersymmetric operators. In the case of extended
supersymmetry, there could be multiple sets: QA
↵ ,w h e r eA =1 ,. . . ,N .
6

=== PDF PAGE 18 ===
gauge bosons. Consequently, the SM fermions should have scalar partner particles, and
the SM gauge bosons should have Weyl fermion partner particles. Additionally, the SM
Higgs boson is expected to reside in a chiral supermultiplet, as scalar ﬁelds can only be
in chiral supermultiplets.
In the low-energy world surrounding us, we have not observed any SUSY partner
particles which have the same mass as the SM particles. Therefore, it is natural to
assume that SUSY is a broken symmetry, as is the case with electroweak symmetry in
the SM. As a result of supersymmetry breaking, SUSY partner particles are expected to
have masses beyond the reach of currect SUSY search experiments.

metric extension of the SM and has been the major target of many studies searching for
hints of SUSY. Table 1.3 shows the particle content of the MSSM. As already mentioned,
each SM fermion has a SUSY partner scalar, and each SM gauge boson has a SUSY
partner fermion.

For the Higgs sector, the MSSM requires an additional Higgs doublet. This is because a
consistent quantum ﬁeld theory requires the cancellation of gauge anomalies, part of which
demands the sum of weak hypercharges for all fermions to be zero. As the fermion partner
of Higgs bosons, the higgsinos, give a non-zero contribution, another Higgs supermultiplet
is required to cancel this contribution. Hence, two Higgs supermultiplets, Hu and Hd,
are introduced, each having hypercharge of YW =1a n d YW = 1. Moreover, two
Higgs supermultiplets are necessary to give correct Yukawa couplings for the SM fermions,
without introducing large ﬂavor-changing neutral currents; Hu gives masses to up-type
quarks, whereas Hd gives masses to down-type quarks and charged leptons.

=== PDF PAGE 19 ===
The MSSM Lagrangian and soft-supersymmetry breaking
One phenomenological motivation of SUSY is to provide a solution to the hierarchy prob-
lem by cancelling the quadratic divergent radiative corrections to the Higgs mass. To
maintain this solution even in the presence of supersymmetry breaking, the idea of “soft”
supersymmetry breaking is introduced. Therefore, the e↵ective Lagrangian of the MSSM
can be written as

In the MSSM, SUSY breaking is introduced explicitly as soft SUSY-breaking terms.
It is worthwhile to mention that this is only an e↵ective way of describing the phe-
nomenological consequences of spontaneous SUSY breaking. Besides, it is presumed that
supersymmetry is spontaneously broken in a “hidden sector” and its e↵ects are “medi-
ated” to the MSSM sector in a non-trivial way. This is because if the origin of SUSY
breaking were in the MSSM sector, this would indicate the existence of a SUSY scalar
particle lighter than its partner SM fermion, which is ruled out by experiment.
There are several viable candidates for models which induce spontaneous supersymme-
try breaking. One is gravity-mediated supersymmetry breaking scenario, which include
the e↵ects of supergravity (SUGRA) [ 65–72]. Supergravity further extends supersym-
metry from a global symmetry to a local symmetry, thus incorporating gravitational
e↵ects. In this case, the gravitino, a SUSY partner fermion of the graviton with spin of
3/2, is introduced. Other candidates of SUSY breaking models include scenarios such as
gauge-mediated supersymmetry breaking (GMSB) [ 73–78]a n da n o m a l y - m e d i a t e ds u p e r -
symmetry breaking (AMSB) [ 79, 80].
8

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===
R-parity
In general, it is possible to include baryon number and lepton number violating terms in
the MSSM Lagrangian. However, such terms could be forbidden by imposing a symmetry
called R-parity:
PR ⌘ (1)3(BL)+2s, (1.18)
where B is the baryon number, L is the lepton number, and s is the spin of the parti-
cle. Consequently, all SM particles have PR = +1 and all SUSY partner particles have
PR = 1. The conservation of R-parity is motivated to prevent the decay of the proton,
the lifetime of which is experimentally constrained to be > 1.6 ⇥ 1034 years [ 47]. There-
fore, the MSSM is deﬁned to conserve R-parity in this dissertation, as is done in many
phenomenological studies of SUSY.
R-parity conservation requires an even number of SUSY particles for each interaction
vertex in the theory. This leads to the following signiﬁcant phenomenological conse-
quences:
• In collider experiments using SM particles, SUSY particles must be produced in
even numbers, usually in pairs.
• The lightest supersymmetric particle (LSP) cannot decay and must be stable.
• SUSY particles except the LSP eventually decay into states that contain an odd
number of LSPs.
1.2.2 Solutions to the remaining problems of the Standard Model
Although SUSY was ﬁrst motivated rather by theoretical aspects of QFT, the MSSM
succeeds in giving fruitful solutions to the remaining problems of the SM.
Dark matter candidates from SUSY
As mentioned in Section 1.2.1,t h el i g h t e s ts u p e r s y m m e t r i cp a r t i c l e( L S P )i nt h eM S S M
is stable. If the LSP is electrically neutral, it interacts only weakly with ordinary matter.
In this case, the LSP is a viable candidate of DM. There are two main candidates of LSP 5:
the lightest neutralino and the gravitino.
As will be discussed later in Sec. 1.2.3,e l e c t r i c a l l yn e u t r a lc o m p o n e n t so fb i n o ,w i n o ,
and higgsino mix to form four mass eigenstates called neutralinos. The lightest neutralino
is a leading candidate of WIMP DM. The mixing ratio of each component for the lightest
neutralino largely depends on the parameters of the MSSM. In the case of bino-like DM,
most models are excluded by the relic density constraint; bino-like DM has very small self
interaction, leading to an early freeze-out and over-abundance of thermal relics. Despite
that, there are scenarios which still survive, for example, the bino-stau coannihilation
scenario, where the coannihilation of bino and the slightly heavier stau, the SUSY partner
of the tau, contribute to annihilation of bino [ 82–84]. In the case of wino-like DM or pure
higgsino-like DM, from the relic density constraint, the LSP mass is allowed up to 3 TeV
5The lightest sneutrino, SUSY partner of the neutrino, could also be a DM candidate, but is largely
excluded by direct detection experiments [ 81]. Further discussion is therefore omitted here.
9

=== PDF PAGE 21 ===
and 1 .1 TeV, respectively. For lower masses, the LSP only accounts partially of all DM;
other types of DM or non-thermal mechanisms must be introduced to explain all dark
matter content [ 85–87]. Nevertheless, it is crucial to experimentally scrutinize all allowed
mass regions, as it is more natural for the LSP to have a mass of O(100) GeV, considering
the hierarchy problem.
On the other hand, the gravitino could be the LSP, which is the likely case for GMSB
scenarios. However, models with gravitino LSP su↵er from cosmological constraints called
the “gravitino problem” [ 88–90]. As a result, it is favored that gravitino LSP should have
mass of . O(10) eV.
Prospects of SUSY GUT
The running of gauge couplings in the MSSM strongly motivates the existence of GUT.
Fig. 1.2 shows the running of the gauge couplings for the SM and the MSSM under
renormalization group equations for SUSY breaking scale of O(TeV). Although the three
gauge couplings do not quite agree for the SM, they agree in the MSSM at ⇤ MSSM ⇠
1016 GeV, indicating the possibility of supersymmetric GUT. Although partially excluded
by proton decay lifetime experiments, viable models of SUSY GUT include SUSY SU (5)
and SUSY SO(10).
Figure 1.2: Running of inverse gauge couplings ↵  1
a (Q)( a =1 , 2, 3) for the SM (dashed lines) and the
MSSM (solid lines) using two-loop renormalization group equations [ 28]. For the MSSM, the common
SUSY mass is varied between 750 GeV and 2 .5 TeV, and ↵ 3(mZ ) is varied between 0.117 and 0.120.

One of the leading motivations of SUSY is its capability of solving the ﬁne-tuning problem
of the Higgs mass. In the SM, the most signiﬁcant correction to the Higgs mass comes
from the one-loop top quark diagram shown in Fig. 1.3a,r e p r e s e n t e da s

As a result of the large third-generation Yukawa couplings and trilinear couplings, the
third generation sfermions could have di↵erent masses compared to their ﬁrst- and second-
generation counterparts. Moreover, there could be considerable mixing of left-handed and
right-handed components for the third generation pairs: ( ˜tL, ˜tR), (˜bL, ˜bR), and (˜⌧L, ˜⌧R). On
the other hand, the ﬁrst- and second- generation sfermions have negligible Yukawa cou-
plings, resulting in nearly degenerate unmixed pairs: (˜ uL, ˜cL), ( ˜dL, ˜sL), (˜uR, ˜cR), ( ˜dR, ˜sR),
(˜eL, ˜µL), (˜eR, ˜µR), and (˜⌫e, ˜⌫µ).

Summary: theory to simpliﬁed models
Although the MSSM consists of a number of fundamental and soft SUSY breaking pa-
rameters, predictive models could be deducted by approximations consistent with various
observations. By adopting approximations Eq. ( 1.38)a n dE q .( 1.39), the MSSM can be

Hints from spontaneous SUSY breaking could also give constraints to the MSSM. In
the case of minimal supergravity model known as mSUGRA, the following relations are
imposed at a renormalization scale Q ⇡ 1.5 ⇥ 1016 GeV:
M1 = M2 = M3 = m1/2, (1.51)

Parameters B0 and µ can be reduced to tan  and sgn( µ)b yr e q u i r i n ge l e c t r o w e a ks y m -
metry breaking. Fig. 1.4 shows the running of mass parameters in mSUGRA. There are
also models which relax the Higgs mass constraint in Eq. ( 1.52), based on the reason-
ing that Higgs mass universality with m0 does not follow any fundamental principles.
These scenarios are known as Non-Universal Higgs Mass (NUHM) models. mSUGRA
and NUHM models only have ﬁve to seven parameters and have been practically used
as target benchmark models for experimental searches. It is worthwhile to note that
mSUGRA scenarios typically predict the LSP to be bino-like, whereas NUHM scenarios
often predict higgsino-like LSP.

1.2.4 Experimental constraints
This section highlights the various experimental constraints to SUSY, with focus on the
MSSM.
Constraints from the Standard Model Higgs mass
The observed Higgs mass of 125 GeV gives a stringent constraint on the MSSM. From
Eq. ( 1.28), one sees that at tree-level,
mh0 <m Z | cos(2)| < 91.2G e V, (1.55)
which is far below the observed mass. Radiative corrections to the Higgs mass mainly
from top and stop loops, give positive contributions, which in principle, are enough to
accomodate the observed mass. The approximate one-loop correction is given by
(m2
h0 )= 3m2
t

where m˜t ⌘ (m˜t1 m˜t2 )1/2 is the general stop mass, and Xt ⌘ At  µ cot  is the stop mixing
parameter [ 91]. For this to be consistent with the observed Higgs mass, the stops must
be much heavier than O(1 TeV) or the stop mixing must be signiﬁcantly large as shown
in Fig. 1.5.
Constraints from collider search experiments
The most “direct” constraints come from high-energy collider searches, performed by
ATLAS and CMS collaborations at the LHC. Fig. 1.6 shows the production cross sections
of various SUSY processes at the LHC.
17

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
Figure 1.5: The Higgs mass in the MSSM as a function of the lightest stop mass m˜t1 [91]. The red(blue)
solid lines show the values computed by Suspect(FeynHiggs). The upper lines show results with maximal
stop mixing Xt =
p
6m˜t, and the lower lines show results with minimal stop mixing Xt = 0.
Gluinos Gluino has high production cross section at the LHC and has been actively
searched for by ATLAS and CMS experiments. Fig. 1.7 shows the exclusion limits for
gluinos set by ATLAS and CMS experiments. Gluino searches are usually performed by
searching for various decay products from the gluino decaying to the LSP, on top of an
energetic jet and high missing transverse momentum. Gluinos with masses up to about
2.4 TeV have been already excluded, depending on the lightest neutralino mass.
Squarks Squarks have also been actively searched by ATLAS and CMS experiments
due to their high production cross sections at LHC. Searches for squarks are performed
separately for third-generation and ﬁrst/second-generation components, because third-
generation squarks tend to be lighter than their ﬁrst- and second-generation counter-
parts, as explained in Section 1.2.3. Fig. 1.8 shows exclusion limits for ﬁrst- and second-
generation squarks set by ATLAS and CMS experiments. Searches for squarks are usually
performed with strategies similar to those of gluino searches, by searching for various de-
cay products from the squarks decaying to the LSP, on top of an energetic jet and high
missing transverse momentum. First- and second-generation squarks with masses up to
about 1.8 TeV have been already excluded, depending on the lightest neutralino mass.
Fig. 1.9 shows exclusion limits for third-generation squarks (stops) set by ATLAS
and CMS experiments. Stops with masses up to 1.3 TeV have been already excluded,
depending on the lightest neutralino mass, ranging up to about 700 GeV. Limits on stop
is less stringent compared to those of ﬁrst- and second-generation squarks, since their
production cross sections are much smaller as shown in Fig. 1.6a.
Electroweak sector Although production cross sections of electroweak SUSY particles
(bino, winos, higgsinos, and sleptons) at the LHC are much smaller than those of strong
SUSY particles, ATLAS and CMS experiments currently lead the SUSY searches tar-
geting the electroweak sector. However, for some parameter regions, results from LEP2
experiments still hold the best results.

Scenarios with wino-like LSP are expected to present “disappearing track” signatures,
which are produced by long-living lightest chargino decaying to the lightest neutralino.
Fig. 1.11 shows exclusion limits for the wino LSP scenario by ATLAS and CMS exper-
iments, targeting disappearing tracks. Pure winos have been excluded up to masses of
650 GeV.
Scenarios with higgsino-like LSP have been explored using several strategies, depend-
ing on the mass splitting values within the higgsino-like triplet ( ˜ 0
1, ˜±
1 , ˜0
2). Fig. 1.12
shows the newest exclusion limits on higgsino-like LSP scenarios set by ATLAS. For pre-
vious searches, multi-lepton searches targeting soft leptons emitted from higgsino decays
have searched regions with  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) larger than about 1.5 GeV. Disappearing track
searches have probed regions with  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) smaller than about 400 MeV, excluding
pure higgsinos with masses of up to about 200 GeV. The newest displaced track search
has successfully searched the region in between, excluding the regions with  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1)
smaller than about 1 GeV, for masses up to about 170 GeV. However, the regions between
those searched by the displaced track search and multi-lepton searches have not yet been
probed by the LHC, leaving the limit by the LEP2 experiments the most stringent. It
is worthwhile to mention here that the purpose of the research described in this
dissertation is to search for higgsino-like LSP scenarios in this regime with
m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) of about 1–1.5 GeV .

Figure 1.12: Recent experimental constraints by ATLAS on the higgsino-like LSP scenarios [ 97]. The

Direct production of sleptons have also been searched at the LHC. Fig. 1.13 shows
the exclusion limits on direct slepton production set by ATLAS. Searches for sleptons
have been commonly performed by focusing on leptons emitted from the ˜l ! l ˜0
1 decay.
Similar to other SUSY searches, events with an energetic jet and high missing transverse
momentum become signiﬁcant for regimes where the slepton and the LSP have compressed
mass spectra. First- and second-generation sleptons (selectrons and smuons) have been
excluded up to masses of about 700 GeV, and third-generation sleptons (staus) have been
excluded up to masses of about 450 GeV, depending on the lightest neutralino mass.

for them. The Higgs sector parameters of the MSSM can be reduced down to mA0 and

Relic density constraint Recent observations indicate the DM relic density to be
⌦DMh2 ' 0.12 [ 13], as described in Section 1.1.2.T h i s v a l u e s e t s a v e r y s t r i n g e n t c o n -
straint on the LSP mass, with the assumption that all dark matter content consists only
of the LSP. However, in principle, it is possible that the LSP only accounts for only a
fraction of the DM content. In this case, the relic density constraint becomes rather loose;
LSP masses below that satisfying the relic density equality are allowed.
Fig. 1.15 shows the expected relic density of the neutralino as a function of the LSP
neutralino mass, for each neutralino type. Bino-like LSP is mostly excluded in terms of
relic density, as it results in overabundance due to its small annihilation cross sections.
On the other hand, wino-like LSP and higgsino-like LSP have relic densities smaller than
the experimental constraint; up to 3 TeV and 1.1 TeV is allowed, respectively. Therefore,
in terms of dark matter relic density, SUSY scenarios with wino-like LSP and higgsino-like
LSP are generally more viable than those with bino-like LSP. However, some bino-like
LSP scenarios are still possible; scenarios where the bino coannihilates with other SUSY
particles (e.g. staus) could realize smaller relic densities [ 103].
Constraints from direct detection experiments Another constraint on WIMP dark
matter comes from direct detection experiments, which search for WIMP-nucleon interac-
tion. The most stringent limit on spin-independent WIMP-nucleon cross section ( SI) 7 is
currently set by the LUX-ZEPLIN (LZ) experiment [ 105]. For WIMP masses of 100 GeV
to 1 TeV, SI down to 2 ⇥ 1047 cm2 to 2 ⇥ 1046 cm2 have already been excluded.
For the projection of direct detection results to constraints on the LSP neutralino,
one must scale the WIMP-nucleon cross section with the expected relic abundance of the
LSP, because it may be the case that the LSP only accounts for a fraction of currently
existing dark matter content. Fig. 1.17 shows the scaled relic density of higgsino-like
7There are also limits on spin-dependent WIMP-nucleon cross section ( SD), but they are less stringent
compared to limits on SI, so the discussion is omitted here.

1.3 Target scenario and search strategy
The search introduced in this dissertation focuses on MSSM scenarios having higgsino-like
LSP. In this section, details of the target scenario and analysis strategies are presented.
1.3.1 Target scenario
MSSM with higgsino-like LSP are one of the most viable candidates of SUSY and BSM
scenarios, from both theoretical and experimental perspectives. LSP neutralino with a
mass of O(100) GeV is a perfect candidate for WIMP dark matter; the options are bino-
like, wino-like, and higgsino-like LSP. Bino-like LSP is mostly excluded due to its small
annihilation cross section and overabundance. Wino-like LSP has already been actively
searched with available LHC data and is excluded up to mass of about 800 GeV, although
depending on the mass splitting. However, higgsino-like LSP has only been searched up
to mass of about 200 GeV with currently available LHC data as shown in Fig. 1.12.F o r
some mass splitting values, limits from LEP2 experiments remain the most stringent,
excluding up to mass of only about 90 GeV. Since solutions to the hierarchy problem
favor SUSY particles to be relatively light ( O(100) GeV), it is highly crucial to search for
higgsino-like LSP in the unfathomed mass regions.
26

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
MSSM scenarios with higgsino-like LSP are also barely constrained from searches
for other SUSY particles described in Section 1.2.4. Higgsino-like LSP scenarios have
typical mass parameter relations of M1,M 2  µ,i n d i c a t i n gl a r g eg l u i n om a s sa c c o r d i n g
to Eq. ( 1.36). Generally, masses of squarks, sleptons, and BSM Higgs could also be much
larger than those excluded by various searches at the LHC. Therefore, MSSM scenarios
with higgsino-like LSP have not been excluded by any indirect experimental constraints
and currently remain as one of the most viable candidates of physics beyond the SM.
In scenarios with higgsino-like LSP, in addition to the lightest neutralino ˜ 0

1.3.3 Analysis strategy
The search presented in this dissertation targets ˜ ±
1 ˜0
2 and ˜0
1 ˜0

2.
The branching ratio of ˜0
2 decaying leptonically to ˜0
1 is approximately 5% for e+e and
µ+µ, respectively. Although the branching ratios are relatively small, targeting this
opposite-charge same-ﬂavor lepton pair is a valid search method, as the features of the
di-lepton system can be exploited to achieve sucient background suppression. How-
ever, for mass splittings of a few GeV, the leptons from ˜ 0
2 ! ˜0
1`+` decay only have

transverse momentum ( pT) of a few GeV. As shown in Fig. 1.21,m a n yo ft h e s el e p t o n s

Fig. 6.1 shows diagrams of the higgsino pair production processes targeted in the
search. The targeted event topologies are similar to those targeted in the previous soft
2L search [ 109]. The lightest neutralino ˜ 0
1 is stable DM candidate in the considered
scenario, thus the two ˜ 0
1 in the ﬁnal state are expected to pass through the detectors
such that their presence is highlighted only by the observation of a non-negligible missing
transverse momentum ( Emiss
T ). In the small mass splitting region, other decay products
typically have very small activities, and the only object which could be used to trigger
these events is the Emiss
T object. However, when the two ˜ 0
1 are emitted back-to-back in
the plane perpendicular to the beampipe, Emiss
T will be small, and the events will fail the
Emiss
T trigger requirement. Therefore, a highly energetic initial state radiation hadronic
jet is required to boost the SUSY system and to align the two ˜ 0
1 to produce large Emiss
T .
To improve sensitivity to regions with  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) = 2–3 GeV, where the soft 2L search
could not probe, the analysis targets events where only one lepton (electron or muon)
from ˜0
2 ! ˜0
1`+` is identiﬁed using the standard identiﬁcation method in ATLAS. The
other lepton is assumed to elude the standard identiﬁcation method, due to its softness.
Therefore, only one standard lepton is required, and the other lepton is only required to
be reconstructed as a track, which is later required to be identiﬁed as a low- pT lepton
track by the newly developed taggers.
Event selection is performed using a state-of-the-art neural network method called
parametrized neural network (pNN) [ 111]. The pNN is optimized to separate signal
higgsino production processes and dominant background processes exploiting the features

method. Dominant contributions are expected to be from W +jets processes, which have

low-pT lepton tagger is likely a misidentiﬁed hadronic track originating from underlying
events. Since there are ambiguities in the modeling of such tracks in the Monte-Carlo
(MC) simulations, these “fake lepton track” contributions are inclusively estimated by a
data-driven method, later described in Section 7. On the other hand, “true lepton track”
contributions, which mainly come from diboson and Z+jets processes are expected to be
well-modeled and are estimated directly using MC simulations.

2. Experimental Setup
The search presented in this dissertation exploits 140 fb 1 of proton-proton collision data
at ps = 13 TeV, collected by the ATLAS experiment in 2015–2018 during LHC Run 2.
This section gives an overview of the LHC and the ATLAS detector.
2.1 Large Hadron Collider
The Large Hadron Collider (LHC) [ 112]i st h ew o r l d ’ sl a r g e s ta n dt h eh i g h e s t - e n e r g y
circular collider, constructed on the borders of France and Switzerland. It has a circum-
ference of 26.659 km and is designed to collide protons at the center-of-mass energy ofps =1 4T e V . T h eb e a m p i p ei ss i t u a t e di nat u n n e la b o u t1 0 0m e t e r su n d e r g r o u n d ,
and the proton collisions occur at four interaction points (IP), where ATLAS [ 1], ALICE
[113], CMS [ 114], and LHCb [ 115] experiments are situated. ATLAS and CMS are large
general-purpose experiments which conduct wide varieties of physics programs. ALICE
and LHCb are more specialized towards studies of heavy-ion collisions and b-hadrons,
respectively.
Table 2.1 summarizes the operation conditions of past and ongoing LHC Runs. The
operation of LHC started in 2010 as “Run 1”, in which data was collected until early 2013
at ps =7 – 8T e V . I nt h eL o n gS h u t d o w n1( L S 1 )p e r i o d ,m a i n t e n a n c e sa n du p g r a d e s
of the accelerator and each detector were carried out. Operation was resumed at ps =
13 TeV in 2015 as “Run 2”, which continued until the end of 2018. After another round
of maintenances and upgrades in Long Shutdown 2 (LS2), the ongoing operation started
in 2022 as “Run 3”, in which the center-of-mass energy is now at ps =1 3.6T e V .

Fig. 2.1 shows a schematic of diagram of the accelerator complex at CERN. Proton
acceleration for the LHC occurs in multiple steps. From 2020, Linear Accelerator 4
33

=== PDF PAGE 45 ===
(LINAC4) has been used for the seeding of protons; it accelerates negative hydrogen ions
to 160 MeV using radiofrequency (RF) cavities. The accelerated ions are then stripped of
their electrons during injection from LINAC4 to the Proton Synchrotron Booster, which
accelerates the protons to 2 GeV. After the protons are accelerated to 26 GeV by the
Proton Synchrotron (PS), they are injected to the Super Proton Synchrotron (SPS) and
accelerated up to 450 GeV. Finally, the protons accelerated by the SPS are injected to
the LHC.
Figure 2.1: A schematic diagram of the CERN accelerator complex [ 116].
Proton beams at the LHC consist of groups of protons called “bunches” and are
accelerated by RF cavities at 400.8 MHz. Since proton bunches are accelerated up to
near the speed of light c,i np r i n c i p l e ,t h e r ec o u l db ea sm u c ha s

bunches in one round of the LHC circumference. However, considering margins for beam
injection and dumping, the LHC is designed to be injected with a maximum of 2808
bunches, having minimum time spacing of 25 ns. Hnece, the frequency of proton bunch
crossings at the LHC is (25 ns) 1 ⇡ 40.08 MHz. Number of protons per bunch has
reached about 1 .6 ⇥ 1011 in 2023, and the mean number of interactions per bunch crossing
is typically larger in Run 3 compared to Run 2 as shown in Fig. 2.2.
The trajectories of proton beams at the LHC are primarily bent by 1232 dipole mag-
nets using superconducting Nb-Ti wires. The dipole magnets are cooled down to 1.9 K

2.2 ATLAS detector
The ATLAS detector is a large general-purpose detector situated at Point 1 of the LHC.
Fig. 2.3 shows the overall view of the detector. The ATLAS detector can be roughly
categorized into three detector groups: Inner Detector, the calorimeters, and the muon
spectrometers. The Inner Detector is in the most inner part of the detector and mea-
sures pT of charged particle tracks. The calorimeters surrounding the Inner Detector are
responsible for identifying electrons, photons, and hadrons, and measuring their energy.
The muon spectrometers consist the outer layers of the detector and measure the pT of
muons. This section gives a brief summary of the magnets, detectors, and Trigger and
Data Acquisition system of ATLAS.
2.2.1 Coordinate system
ATLAS experiment adopts a right-handed Cartesian coordinate system shown in Fig. 2.4.
In the Cartesian coordinates, the x-axis points towards the center of the LHC ring, the
y-axis points upward, and the origin is at the center of the detector, where the beam
collisions occur. In terms of the cylindrical coordinate system, the radial distance R and
azimuth angle  are also used. Moreover, using the polar angle ✓, pseudorapidity ⌘ is
deﬁned as
⌘ =  ln

Figure 2.3: Overall view of the ATLAS detector [ 1]. The cylindrical detector is 44 m long and 25 m in
diameter and weighs 7000 t.
which is convenient for describing detector coverage and particle orientation. The region
corresponding to the side of the cylinder, is referred to as the “barrel” region, and that
corresponding to the base is referred to as the “endcap” region.
Figure 2.4: Coordinate system of the ATLAS detector. The x-axis points towards the center of the LHC
ring, and the y-axis points upward, towards the sky. The side with z> 0 is referred to as the A-side, and
the side with z< 0 is referred to as the C-side. ⌘ is often used to describe the coverage of the detectors.
2.2.2 Magnet system
The ATLAS detector has three types of superconducting magnets, which are necessary
for particle identiﬁcation and their pT measurement. Fig. 2.5 shows a schematic diagram
of the ATLAS magnet system. The system consists of three types of magnets: central
solenoid, barrel toroid, and endcap toroid.
36

=== PDF PAGE 48 ===
Figure 2.5: Schematic diagram of the ATLAS magnet system [ 1]. The system consists of three types of
magnets: central solenoid, barrel toroid, and endcap toroid.
Central solenoid
The central solenoid magnet is situated between the Inner Detector and the Liquid Argon
calorimeter (later described in Sections 2.2.3 and 2.2.4)a n di sn e c e s s a r yf o rb e n d i n g
the trajectories of charged particles within the Inner Detector. The magnetic ﬁeld of
the central solenoid is approximately 2 T. Its length is 5.8 m, and its inner and outer
diameters are 2.46 m and 2.56 m, respectively.
Barrel toroid
Eight barrel toroid magnets are situated in the barrel regions, in the outer regions with
muon systems. These are used to bend trajectories of muons in the barrel regions for pT
measurement. The length of the barrel toroid is 25.3 m, and its inner and outer diameters
are 9.4 m and 20.1 m, respectively.
Endcap toroid
Two endcap toroid magnets are situated in the endcap regions, one on each side of the
detector. These are used to bend trajectories of muons in the endcap regions for momen-
tum measurement. Each endcap toroid has eight coils, each placed between the barrel
toroid coils. Its axial length is 5.0 m, and its inner and outer diameters are 1.65 m and
10.7 m, respectively.
2.2.3 Inner Detector
The ATLAS Inner Detector performs various tracking measurements of charged particles,
such as impact parameters, pT, and charge. As shown in Fig. 2.6,t h eI n n e rD e t e c t o r
consists of three subdetectors: Pixel, Semiconductor Tracker (SCT), and Transition Ra-
diation Tracker (TRT).
37

=== PDF PAGE 49 ===
(a) Cutaway view
 (b) Cross section view
Figure 2.6: Schematic views of the ATLAS Inner Detector [ 118, 119]. The Inner Detector consists of
three subdetectors: Pixel, SCT, and TRT.
Pixel
The Pixel detector is structured from four layers of silicon pixel sensors. The most inner
layer at R =3 3.25 mm is called the Insertable B-Layer (IBL) and was newly introduced in
Run 2. The position resolution of the silicon pixels in the IBL is (  , z )=( 8 µm, 40 µm).
The three outer layers used from Run 1 are each called layer-0, layer-1, and layer-2 in
the barrel regions and disks in the endcap regions. The position resolution of the silicon
pixels in these layers is (  , z(R))=( 1 0 µm, 115 µm). Pixel covers regions with |⌘| < 2.5.
Semiconductor Tracker
The Semiconductor Tracker (SCT) detector is made of layers of silicon strip sensors. The
barrel and endcap regions each consist of four and nine layers, respectively. Each layer has
two stereo strips with 40 mrad angle di↵erence to realize 2D stereo measurement. The
position measurement of the 2D stereo measurement is (  , z(R))=( 1 7 µm, 580 µm).
SCT covers regions with |⌘| < 2.5.
Transition Radiation Tracker
The Transition Radiation Tracker (TRT) is made of straw-shaped drift tubes with a
diameter of 4 mm. The barrel tubes are placed in parallel with the LHC beampipe,
whereas the endcap tubes are oriented in the R-direction. TRT tubes are placed in a
way that all particles in |⌘| < 2.0( e x c l u d i n gt h eb a r r e l - e n d c a pg a pi n0.8 < |⌘| < 1.0)
pass through at least 36 tubes; by combination with hits provided by Pixel and SCT at
small radii, it is possible to reconstruct particle tracks with high precision. The position
resolution of TRT itself is 130 µm per straw. Although Pixel and SCT have detector
coverage of |⌘| < 2.5, TRT only covers regions with |⌘| < 2.0.
TRT also plays a signiﬁcant role in electron identiﬁcation. When charged particle
tracks pass through the radiator material between the tubes, photons emitted by transition
38

=== PDF PAGE 50 ===
radiation are absorbed by the xenon gas in the drift tubes. As subsequent ionization
proceeds, the transition radiation is detected through the anode wires centered in the drift
tubes. Since the probability of transition radiation increases with the relativistic gamma
factor, for particles with energy less than 100 GeV, only electrons are capable of inducing
transition radiation within the ATLAS detector. Therefore, electron identiﬁcation in
ATLAS is enhanced by checking the presence of transition radiation in the TRT.
2.2.4 Calorimeters
Fig. 2.7 shows a cutaway view of the ATLAS calorimeter detectors. Calorimeters in
ATLAS are roughly categorized into two types by their usage: electromagnetic calorime-
ters and hadronic calorimeters. Moreover, they can also be categorized by their active
material: Liquid Argon (LAr) calorimeters and Tile calorimeters.
Figure 2.7: Cutaway view of the ATLAS calorimeter detectors [ 1]. The calorimeters are roughly catego-
rized into two types by their usage: electromagnetic and hadronic.
Electromagnetic calorimeters
The electromagnetic (EM) calorimeters measure the position and energy of electrons and
photons. LAr electromagnetic barrel and endcap calorimeters in Fig. 2.7 are the two EM
calorimeters. Since LAr calorimeters operate at 88.5 K, these calorimeters are housed in
cryostats. EM calorimeters have accordion-like structures made from lead as absorber
material and liquid argon as active material as shown in Fig. 2.8.T h e s e s t r u c t u r e s e n a b l e
full coverage in the -direction.
The EM barrel calorimeter covers |⌘| < 1.475 and has a total thickness of at least 22 X0,
where X0 is the radiation length, thus assuring sucient absorption of EM showers. As
shown in Fig. 2.8,t h eE Mb a r r e lc a l o r i m e t e rc o n s i s t so ft h r e el a y e r s ,e a c hh a v i n gd i ↵ e r e n t
granularities (cell sizes). Moreover, there is an additional layer called the presampler
before the ﬁrst layer for correcting the electron and photon energy lost by upstream of
the calorimeter.

Hadronic calorimeters
Hadronic calorimeters measure the position and energy of hadrons, primarily hadronic
jets. Fig. 2.9 shows the cumulative amount of material of each calorimeter in units of
hadronic interaction length. The hadronic calorimeters are designed to have sucient
containment of hadronic showers and also to reduce punch-through of hadrons into the
muon spectrometers. Hadronic calorimeters are classiﬁed into three types: Tile calorime-
ters, LAr hadronic endcap calorimeters, and LAr forward calorimeters.
Figure 2.9: Cumulative amount of material in the ATLAS calorimeters in units of hadronic interaction

ﬁrst active layer of the muon spectrometers (up to |⌘| < 3.0).
Tile calorimeters are made from steel as absorber material and scintillator tiles as ac-
tive material. Tile barrel calorimeter covers regions with |⌘| < 1.0, and the Tile extended
barrel covers regions with 0 .8 < |⌘| < 1.7. The thickness of the Tile calorimeters corre-
sponds to 7 .40,w h e r e0 is the hadronic interaction length. Tile barrel and extended
barrel are both divided into 64 modules in the -direction as shown in Fig. 2.10.
LAr hadronic endcap calorimeters are placed in the endcap regions, outside the EM
endcap calorimeters, covering 1 .5 < |⌘| < 3.2. Instead of lead, LAr hadronic endcap
calorimeters adopts copper as their absorber material. The granularity of LAr hadronic
endcap calorimeters is  ⌘ ⇥  =0 .1 ⇥ 0.1f o r1 .5 < |⌘| < 2.5a n d ⌘ ⇥  =0 .2 ⇥ 0.2
for 2 .5 < |⌘| < 3.2.
LAr forward calorimeters cover the forward regions of ATLAS, in particular, regions
with 3 .1 < |⌘| < 4.9. The forward calorimeter, consisting of three layers, is used as both
EM and hadronic calorimeters; the ﬁrst layer is used as an EM calorimeter, and the second
and third layers are used as hadronic calorimeters. As for the absorber material, the ﬁrst
layer adopts copper, and the second and third layer adopt tungsten.

2.2.5 Muon spectrometers
The muon spectrometers consist the most outer layers of the ATLAS detector and have
the signiﬁcant role of reconstruction and identiﬁcation of muons. Although the muon
detectors have been partially upgraded for the ongoing Run 3, the conﬁgurations for Run
2 are summarized in this section, as the search presented in this paper uses Run 2 data.
Fig. 2.11 shows the overall view of the ATLAS muon spectrometers, which consist
of four subdetectors: Monitored Drift Tube (MDT), Cathode Strip Chamber (CSC),
Resistive Plate Chamber (RPC), and Thin Gap Chamber (TGC). MDT and CSC have
high position resolution and are responsible for precision pT measurements of muons.
RPC and TGC have high timing resolution and provide muon trigger signals.
Monitored Drift Tube
Monitored Drift Tube (MDT) is a precision measurement muon detector and consists of
three layers of drift chambers for both barrel and endcap regions. The coverage is |⌘| < 2.0
for the most inner layer and |⌘| < 2.7f o rt h eo u t e rt w ol a y e r s .
Fig. 2.12a shows the structure of the MDT drift tube. Each drift tube is made of
aluminum and is about 30 mm in diameter. When a muon passes through the tube, the
AR/CO2 gas is ionized, and the resulting electrons drift towards the anode wire, with a
maximum drifting time of 700 ns. The position resolution of each tube is estimated to
be 80 µm. Fig. 2.12b shows the MDT chamber structure. Each chamber has one or more
“multilayers”, which consist of 3–4 layers of drift tubes. Although the chamber frames
have robust structures made of aluminum, a laser-based alignment system is implemented
42

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===
Figure 2.11: Overall view of the ATLAS muon spectrometers [ 1]. Four detectors consist the muon system:
MDT, CSC, RPC, and TGC.
to detect distortions from aging.
Cathod Strip Chamber
Cathode Strip Chamber (CSC) is a multiwire proportional chamber speciﬁcally made for
performing muon precision measurements in the endcap regions with 2 .0 < |⌘| < 2.7.
Although the MDT chambers also cover the same regions, muon hit frequency in the
endcap regions exceeds the stable operation threshold of MDT chambers of 150 Hz /cm2.
Hence, the CSC was prepared complementarily for precision measurement of muons in
the endcap regions.
Fig. 2.13a shows the layout of CSC chambers. There are 16 chambers for each CSC
wheel: eight large and eight small chambers. In each chamber, anode wires are placed in
the radial direction with spacings of 2.5 mm, and cathode strips are placed perpendicularly
in the -direction, as shown in Fig. 2.13b. When a muon passes through the chamber,
the AR /CO2 gas is ionized, resulting in an electron avalanche. The position of the muon
is determined by reading out the charge distribution on the cathode strips. The position
resolution is 60 µm per CSC plane, and the timing resolution is 7 ns, suciently high to
withstand high muon hit frequency in the endcap regions.
The CSC was decommissioned after Run 2 and has been upgraded to the New Small
Wheel (NSW) detector in the ongoing Run 3.
Resistive Plate Chamber
Resisitive Plate Chamber (RPC) is a gaseous parallel electrode-plate detector, designed
to provide muon trigger signals in |⌘| < 1.05. RPC consists of three stations, each of
which has a structure shown in Fig. 2.14.E a c h s t a t i o n h a s t w o u n i t s , e a c h h a v i n g t w o
gas layers for measuring ( ⌘, )o ft h em u o nh i tp o s i t i o n .

The chambers of RPC consist of a 2 mm thick gas layer sandwiched by two highly
resistive plastic plates. An electron avanlanche is induced when a muon passes through
the nonﬂammable gas mixture (94.7% C 2H2F4,5 . 0 %I s o - C4H10,0 . 3 %S F6), with applied
voltage of 4.9 kV/mm. The position resolution of RPC is 10 mm in both z-a n d -
directions, and the timing resolution is 1.5 ns, suciently high to operate as a trigger
detector.
Thin Gap Chamber
Thin Gap Chamber (TGC) is a multiwire proportional chamber detector, which provides
muon trigger signals in the endcap regions with 1 .05 < |⌘| < 2.4. TGC is roughly
categorized into two types: TGC Big Wheel (TGC-BW) and TGC Inner (TGC-I) situated
farther from and closer to the IP relative to the endcap toroid magnet, respectively.
Fig. 2.15a shows the gas chamber structure of TGC. The anode wires have spacings
of 1.8 mm, and the cathode strips are placed 1.4 mm apart from the wires, resulting
in a 2.8 mm thick gas layer. When a muon passes through the gas mixture (55% CO 2,
45% n-C5H12), an electron avalanche is induced by an applied voltage of 2.8 kV. The
wires detect the positive ions caused by the avalanche, and the cathode strips detect the
image charges of those ions. The position resolution of each chamber is 2–6 mm in the
R-direction and 3–7 mm in the -direction, and its timing resolution is about 4 ns.
TGC-BW and TGC-I both consist of multiple stations, each having two or three gas
layers, as shown in Fig. 2.15b. The station with three and two gas layers are called triplets
and doublets, respectively. TGC-BW have three stations, one triplet and two doublets,
which are inclusively used to measure the pT of muons. TGC-I have two doublet stations
called Endcap Inner (EI) and Forward Inner (FI), each covering di↵erent ⌘ regions. The
Inner stations are primarily used to veto muon candidates which do not come from the
IP. However, similar to the CSC, TGC-FI has been decommissioned after Run 2 and has
been replaced by the NSW detector.

 (b) Cross-section view of TGC triplet and doublet stations

2.2.6 Luminosity detectors
Precise measurement of integrated luminosity is necessary for properly normalizing the
simulated dataset for comparison with collected data. The integrated luminosity of the
data used in the search presented in this paper has been measured by the luminosity
detectors of ATLAS.
Instantaneous luminosity L is calculated with
L = µnbf
 , (2.2)
where nb is the number of colliding bunches, f =1 1 .246 kHz is the frequency of LHC
proton bunch revolution,  is the cross section of inelastic proton-proton collisions, and µ is
the number of proton-proton interactions per bunch crossing, averaged over all colliding
bunches [ 120]. Although  = 80 mb is assumed in all experiments at the LHC, the
instantaneous luminosity is measured independent of that assumption; L is calculated
by measuring the visible interaction rate per bunch µvis and the visible cross section vis
using the following luminosity detectors.
LUCID
LUCID is a luminosity detector situated in the forward regions of the ATLAS detector,
at z = ±17 m from the IP. LUCID consists of 16 photonmultiplier tubes surrounding the
beampipe, which detect Cherenkov light emitted by charged particles passing through
the C 4F10 gas ﬁlled in the tubes. µvis is then measured online by counting the number
of Cherenkov light signals, each corresponding to one or more inelastic proton-proton
collisions. On the other hand, vis is measured during special LHC ﬁlls with the van der
Meer (vdM) scan method. LUCID was upgraded to LUCID-2 [ 121]a tt h es t a r to fR u n2
to cope with increased energy and higher luminosity conditions.
Beam Conditions Monitor
Beam Conditions Monitor (BCM) detector is at z = ±1.84 m from the IP and consists
of four diamond sensors. BCM mainly provides beam conditions information and beam
46

=== PDF PAGE 58 ===
abort functionalities for protecting the ATLAS inner detector. It was the main lumi-
nosity detector in Run 1, however, its use was limited to consistency checks for special
conﬁguration runs in Run 2, due to technical problems such as radiation damage.
2.2.7 Trigger and Data Acquisition system
As mentioned in Section 2.1, proton bunch crossings at the LHC occur at 40 MHz, and
50–60 proton interactions occur in each bunch crossing. However, target processes of
interest such as physics beyond the SM and Higgs production, have cross sections much
smaller compared to those of proton-proton inelastic scattering. Therefore, a sophisticated
Trigger and Data Acquisition (TDAQ) system is necessary to select and record data
with physics of interest, making ecient use of limited readout bandwidths and o✏ine
computing resources. Fig. 2.16 shows the overview of the ATLAS TDAQ system in LHC
Run 2. ATLAS adopts a two-step trigger system consisting of the hardware-based Level-1
Trigger and the software-based High Level Trigger.
Figure 2.16: Overview of the ATLAS TDAQ system in LHC Run 2 [ 122]. On the left is the trigger
system, and on the right is the DAQ system.
Level-1 Trigger
The Level-1 Trigger is a hardware-based trigger system, which reduces the data rate
from 40 MHz to about 100 kHz using calorimeter and muon information. In the Level-
1c a l o r i m e t e r( L 1 C a l o )t r i g g e r ,a n a l o gs i g n a l sf r o mt h ec a l o r i m e t e r sa r eﬁ r s td i g i t i z e d
and calibrated by the preprocessor and then sent to the Cluster Processor (CP) and
Jet/Energy-sum Processor (JEP). The CP system identiﬁes electron, photon, and tau
candidates, whereas the JEP system identiﬁes hadronic jet candidates and calculates
the total jet energy sum and missing transverse energy. In the Level-1 muon (L1Muon)
trigger, muon candidates are identiﬁed in the barrel and endcap regions using muon hit
information from RPC and TGC detectors, respectively. Based on the information from
47

=== PDF PAGE 59 ===
L1Calo and L1Muon triggers, Central Trigger Processor (CTP) forms the ﬁnal L1 trigger
decision. Data corresponding to the L1 trigger decision is then read out from the detector
frontend systems to the ATLAS readout system. The Level-1 Trigger is required to operate
with a ﬁxed latency within 2 .5 µs.
High Level Trigger
The High Level Trigger (HLT) is a software-based trigger system, which further reduces
the data rate to 1–2 kHz using more precise and CPU-intesive algorithms, motivated by
physics of interest. For event data read out to the ATLAS readout system, the HLT per-
forms online reconstruction based on Region-of-Interest (RoI) information passed on from
the Level-1 Trigger. The HLT decision is processed within a few hundred milliseconds.
Data Acquisition
In all detector subsystems, data for every bunch crossing is temporarily stored in the
detector frontends. Subsequently, only the corresponding data accepted by the Level-
1T r i g g e ri sf u r t h e rr e a do u tt ot h eR e a d o u tD r i v e r s( R O D s ) ,i nw h i c he v e n td a t ai s
organized into a common format. The formatted data is then passed to the ATLAS
central Readout System (ROS), which is responsible for bu↵ering event data from all
detector subsystems and transferring them to the HLT system. Event data accepted by
the HLT is sent to permanent storage via the Sub-Farm Output (SFO).

3.3 Monte-Carlo sample simulation
This section highlights the simulated event samples for signal higgsino production pro-
cesses and background processes used in the analysis. The simulation is done using
Monte-Carlo (MC) methods; the simulated event samples are hereinafter referred to as
MC samples in this dissertation.
3.3.1 Signal MC sample generation
As mentioned in Section 1.3.1,t h es e a r c hp r e s e n t e di nt h i sd i s s e r t a t i o na s s u m e sas i m p l i -
ﬁed higgsino-like LSP model, a typical case where the LSP is dominated by the higgsino
component. In such a model, the masses of the higgsino triplet ( ˜ 0
2,˜±
1 ,˜0
1) have the
following relations:
• m(˜0

• m(˜±
1 )= 1

2)+ m(˜0

.
Thus, the signal MC samples have been generated with the above assumptions. All other
SUSY particles are assumed to be much heavier and not have any phenomenological rela-
tions with the higgsinos. Total of 28 mass points have been considered to target the unex-
plored regions: the considered mass splitting values are  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1)=1 .0, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0,
and 5 .0 GeV, and the considered masses are m(˜0
1)=8 5 , 100, 125, 150, and 175 GeV.
Fig. 3.2 shows an overall view of the mass points used for signal MC sample generation,
compared to the exclusion limits set by other searches. For each of the mass points, sep-
arate MC samples have been generated to properly calculate the cross sections for each
of the three production processes: ˜ 0

The signal MC samples have been generated at leading order (LO) with MG5 aMC@NLO
2.9.3 [124] using the NNPDF23LO [ 125] parton distribution function (PDF) set. The spin
of the higgsinos were conserved using MadSpin [126]. The events were then interfaced
with Pythia 8.245 [127]t om o d e lp a r t o ns h o w e r i n g ,h a d r o n i z a t i o n ,a n du n d e r l y i n ge v e n t s
using A14 tune [ 128]. The matching between the matrix element and parton shower was
performed using the CKKW-L scheme [ 129], with the merging scale set to 15 GeV.
For ecient generation of the targeted event topology, several requirements have been
imposed during the generation step. First, due to the ISR topology targeted by this search,
the samples are generated only for events associated with at least one hadronic jet with
pT > 50 GeV. The e↵ect of this requirement on the production cross section is considered
by applying a dedicated correction factor, later described in this section. Moreover, as an
event ﬁlter, the transverse component of the momentum sum of non-interacting particles

contribution, such as t¯t,s i n g l et o p ,d i j e t ,a n d+jets, have also been included just in case,

generated with Sherpa 2.2.2 but partially generated with Sherpa 2.2.1.

3.3.3 Detector and pileup simulation
All signal and background MC samples are processed through the full ATLAS simula-
tion framework [ 140], which uses Geant4 [141]. Simulation of the e↵ects of pileup is
performed by generating soft QCD processes separately and overlaying them to each of
the hard-scatter events. The MC samples are then reweighted to have a matching pileup
distribution with observed data, with respect to the number of pileup through the data-
taking periods.
53

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
4. Standard Object Reconstruction and
Identiﬁcation
For both recorded data and simulated MC samples, detector-level information is processed
and translated into physics-level “objects” through reconstruction and identiﬁcation. This
section summarizes the reconstruction and identiﬁcation of standard objects used in this
search.
4.1 Tra ck
The trajectories of charged particles are reconstructed as tracks using the hit information
in the Inner Detectors. The tracks used in this search are standard tracks reconstructed
by the primary “inside out” algorithm [ 142]. For each layer of Pixel and SCT detectors,
the algorithm ﬁrst combines signals into clusters, which are further converted to three
dimensional “space points”. Next, track seeds are formed from three space points, ex-
pected to be originating from the same charged track trajectory. Track seeds are then
extended through a combinatorial Kalman ﬁlter [ 143], which yield a set of potential track
candidates. Since these candidates still have overlaps, an ambiguity resolution algorithm
and a subsequent global 2 ﬁt method are applied to obtain the ﬁnal tracks.
After the ﬁnal track candidates are obtained from the Pixel and SCT hits, extensions
to the TRT detector is attempted. TRT extension is performed in a similar way using
aK a l m a nﬁ l t e ra n dag l o b a l2 ﬁt. At least nine TRT hits are required to perform the
extension, and the extension may be rejected when the ﬁt quality is detrimentally a↵ected.
Tracks are describ ed by ﬁve parameters using a p erigee representation as shown in
Fig. 4.1. d0 and z0 are the transverse and longitudinal impact parameters with respect
to the primary vertex, described later in Section 4.2.  and ✓ are the azimuth and polar
angles similar to those deﬁned in Section 2.2.1. q/p is the charge of the track divided
by the magnitude of its momentum, measured from the curvature of the track under the
solenoid magnetic ﬁeld.
Preselection requirements in this search
The following preselection requirements, deﬁned as the TightPrimary working point, are
imposed to the tracks used in the analysis.
• pT > 500 MeV
• |⌘| < 2.5

4.2 Vertex
The positions where the proton-proton collisions occurred, often referred to as the primary
vertices, must be reconstructed for each event to ﬁnd the “hard-scatter” vertex and provide
ar e f e r e n c ep o i n tf o ro b j e c tr e c o n s t r u c t i o n . P r i m a r yv e r t e xr e c o n s t r u c t i o ni sp e r f o r m e d
using vertex ﬁnding and ﬁtting algorithms, exploiting reconstructed track information
[144].
Av e r t e xs e e di sﬁ r s tf o u n df r o mt h em a x i m u mi nt h ez coordinate distribution of the
reconstructed tracks. Then, the vertex seed and its surrounding tracks are used in the
adaptive vertex ﬁtting algorithm [ 145] for determining the vertex position. Tracks incom-
patible with the found vertex is used to seed another vertex, and the ﬁnding procedure
is repeated until all tracks are associated to vertices, each required to have at least two
associated tracks. Out of the multiple primary vertices found by these procedures, one
vertex with the highest sum of associated track pT is considered as the “hard-scatter”
vertex and is used as the reference point of object calibrations.
4.3 Topo-cluster
Particle energy measurement in the calorimeters are performed for each three-dimensional
“topo-cluster”, which is a cluster of topologically connected calorimeter cell signals [ 146].
The formation of topo-clusters is performed by a three dimensional growing-volume al-
gorithm starting from a seed cell with high energy deposit. Neighboring cells of the seed
cell are added to the cluster if the cell signal has a energy deposit exceeding two times
the expected noise signal. The neighboring cells are then considered as the seed cells
for the next iteration of the growth. The growth continues until the all cells exceeding
the threshold are collected. If two growing clusters overlap, they are merged into one
cluster. The cluster formed by the this algorithm could be too large to provide precise
measurement of the particle energy. Hence, cluster splitting is applied if there are two or
more local signal maxima deﬁned by Ecell > 500 MeV.
56

=== PDF PAGE 68 ===
4.4 Jet
Reconstruction and calibration
Gluons and quarks produced by the proton-proton interactions are reconstructed as groups
of hadrons called “jets” as a result of hadronization. Jets are reconstructed using charged
tracks and topo-clusters with dedicated algorithms. First using the particle ﬂow algorithm
[147], tracks and topo-clusters are matched accordingly, and cell-based energy subtraction
is performed to remove overlaps between the momentum and energy measurements in
the Inner Detector and calorimeters. Next, using the tracks originating from the hard-
scatter vertex and topo-clusters surviving the energy subtraction in particle ﬂow, jets are
reconstructed using the anti- kt algorithm [ 148]w i t hr a d i u sp a r a m e t e rR =0 .4.
Since the energy of the jets are reconstructed at electromagnetic scale, jet energy scale
calibration [ 149] is performed to correct the measured jet energy. Fig. 4.2 shows the
stages of jet energy scale calibrations. Pileup corrections are ﬁrst conducted to subtract
contributions of pileup from the jet pT. This is followed by the absolute MC-based cali-
bration, which corrects the reconstructed jet four-momentum to the particle-level energy
scale, considering detector response e↵ects. Global sequential calibration is later per-
formed to reduce dependencies on the initiating and constituent particles. Finally, in situ
calibrations are applied to data to correct for the di↵erences between data and MC.
Figure 4.2: Stages of jet energy scale calibrations [ 149].
Jet vertex tagging
Pileup mitigation is necessary to suppress jets which do not originate from the hard-
scatter event. For the jets used in the analysis, jet vertex tagging (JVT) [ 150]i sa p p l i e d
for this purpose. JVT is constructed using a two-dimensional likelihood in the corrected
jet vertex fraction (corrJVF) and RpT plane. The corrJVF variable is deﬁned as the
scalar pT sum of the tracks associated with the jet and originate from the hard-scatter
vertex (PV 0), divided by the scalar pT sum of the associated tracks that originate from
all interactions including pileup [ 150]:

Flavor tagging
Jet ﬂavor tagging plays a signiﬁcant role in identifying physics processes. In particular,
separation of jets containing b-hadrons and c-hadrons ( b-a n d c-jets) from light-ﬂavor jets
(u-, d-, and s-jets) and gluon jets is of high importance. In this search, ﬂavor tagging is
used to veto events with b-jets to suppress t¯t backgrounds.
Flavor tagging algorithms exploit the long lifetime of b-a n d c-hadrons, generally re-
sulting in a displaced secondary vertex, few mm away from the hard-scatter primary
vertex. First, low-level algorithms reconstruct the characteristics of b-and c-jets using
properties of tracks and displaced vertices. IP2D and IP3D [151]a r el o w - l e v e la l g o r i t h m s
which use the impact parameters of individual tracks associated with the jets. RNNIP
algorithm [ 152] uses recurrent neural networks (RNNs) to further consider the correla-
tion of impact parameters between tracks; if one track has a large impact parameter, it
is likely that other associated tracks also have large impact parameters. SV1 [153]i sa
secondary vertex tagging algorithm that reconstructs a displaced secondary vertex within
aj e t ,u s i n ga2 test. JetFitter [154]u s e sam o d i ﬁ e dK a l m a nﬁ l t e r[143]t or e c o n s t r u c t
the full b-a n d c-hadron decay chain by exploiting the topological features within the jet.
58

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
The subsequent high-level algorithm combines the outputs of the low-level algorithms
using multivariate classiﬁers. This search uses the DL1r algorithm [ 155], which combines
the results of IP2D, IP3D, RNNIP, SV1,a n d JetFitter, using a fully connected NN. The
DL1r NN outputs the probabilities for a jet to be a b-jet, a c-jet, or a light-ﬂavor jet,
deﬁned as pb, pc,a n d plight,r e s p e c t i v e l y .
This search uses b-jet tagging to veto events with b-jets, mainly to suppress t¯t processes.

In searches using leading jets, imposing certain criteria on the quality of the jets help

are known as jet cleaning.

4.5 Electron
When electrons pass through material, they radiate photons through Bremsstrahlung, and
the generated photons create electrons through pair production, resulting in an electro-
magnetic (EM) shower. This mainly occurs in the EM calorimeter in the ATLAS detector,
but could also occur more upstream, inside the Inner Detector. Thus, the reconstruction
of electrons is performed using track and EM cluster information. Fig. 4.5 shows the path
of an electron inside the ATLAS detector.
Figure 4.5: Schematic illustration of the electron path within the ATLAS detector [ 157].
Electron reconstruction starts o↵ with seed-cluster reconstruction in the EM calorime-
ter. This is done using a sliding-window algorithm [ 158], which seeds energy cluster
candidates from localized energy deposits by scanning over the whole EM calorimeter.
The seed-cluster candidates are then matched to modiﬁed tracks, which account for the
energy loss in material through an optimzed Gaussian-sum ﬁlter (GSF) [ 159]. Finally,
reconstructed clusters are formed around the seed clusters, and energy calibration is per-
formed using multivariate algorithms [ 160].
Identiﬁcation of electrons are performed using a likelihood-based (LH) method [ 157],
which uses information from tracking, calorimetry, and combinations of both as inputs.
The ﬁnal discriminant for identiﬁcation is formed by transforming the signal likelihood
60

inant, three working points are deﬁned for electrons with ET > 4.5 GeV, as shown in

4.6 Muon
Muon reconstruction is mainly based on information from the tracks in the Inner Detector
and the measurements in the muon spectrometers [ 162, 163]. The matching between
tracks in the Inner Detector and the tracks in the muon spectrometers is performed by a
combined track ﬁt based on the hits in both Inner Detector and muon spectrometers, also
considering the energy loss in the calorimeters. This is done using an outside-in approach,
61

=== PDF PAGE 73 ===
in which a muon reconstructed in the muon spectrometers is extrapolated inwards and
matched to a track in the Inner Detector.
Muons are identiﬁed by imposing various requirements on features such as the number
of hits in the Inner Detector and muon spectrometers, track ﬁt properties, and variables
deﬁned to test the compatibilities between individual measurements in the Inner Detector
and muon spectrometers. For instance, the q/p compatibility [ 163], deﬁned as
q/p compatibility = |q/pID  q/pMS|p
2(q/pID)+ 2(q/pMS)

is required to be small for muons, where q/pID(MS) and (q/pID(MS))r e p r e s e n tt h eq/p
and its uncertainty measured in the Inner Detector (muon spectrometers), respectively.
