4.7 Overlap removal
Since the reconstruction of multiple physics objects are performed individually, this could
potentially result in ambiguities and overlaps between objects. T o avoid the “double-
counting” of each object, these ambiguities are resolved by performing sequential proce-
dures known as “overlap removal (OR)”. The removal procedures are carried out in the fol-
lowing order, using the angular distance between objects deﬁned as dR =
p
(⌘)2 +(  )2.
Note that overlap removals for photons and tau-leptons are not applied, because they are
not explicitly used in the event selection described in Section 6.
Muon-electron OR
Reject the muon if its Inner Detector track is shared with an electron.
Jet-electron OR
Reject the jet if not a b-jet and dR < 0.2.
Electron-jet OR
Reject the electron if dR < min(0.4, 0.04 + 10 GeV /pT(e)).
Jet-muon OR
Reject the jet if not a b-jet and dR < 0.2.
Muon-jet OR
Reject the muon if dR < min(0.4, 0.04 + 10 GeV /pT(µ)).
4.8 Missing transverse momentum
Particles that do not interact with strong or electromagnetic force will likely pass through
the ATLAS detector without leaving any trace. These include SM particles such as neu-
trinos and BSM particles such as neutralinos. However, since the transverse momentum
of the initial p-p collision system at the LHC is zero, they can be indirectly detected as
missing transverse momentum pmiss
T with its magnitude Emiss
T .
Missing transverse momentum is reconstructed as the negative vector sum of all visible
objects, which consist of hard and soft objects. The hard objects are comprised of elec-
trons ( e), photons ( ), hadronically decaying tau leptons ( ⌧had), muons ( µ), and jets ( j).
The term considering soft objects, often referred to as the soft term, consider additional
contributions from charged tracks which are from the hard-scatter vertex and are unused
in the reconstruction of other objects. Thus, the missing transverse momentum can be
written as [ 164]

5.1.1 Identiﬁcation algorithm
The new low- pT electron track identiﬁcation (hereinafter referred to as LPET ID) targets
tracks having pT down to 0.5 GeV, which is the lower bound required for charged tracks.
Considering that some of the electrons, having pT larger but close to the 4.5 GeV threshold,
escape the standard electron ID, the new LPET ID targets tracks having pT =0 .5–5 GeV.
Tracks in the target pT range is dominated by hadrons (mostly charged pions) and fake
tracks. The idea of LPET ID is to distinguish electron-like tracks from these background
tracks by exploiting their characteristics. The characteristics of tracks can be roughly
categorized into two groups: silicon (Pixel and SCT) detector response and TRT detector
response. Since the detector response of TRT has a large impact on electron identiﬁcation
as described in Section 2.2.3,i t si n f o r m a t i o ni sa c t i v e l yu s e df o rt r a c k sw i t hs u c c e s s f u l
extension. In addition to this tracking information, energy deposits in the electromagnetic
calorimeter are used by matching each track to the corresponding topo-cluster. LPET
ID has been designed to utilize these track and calorimeter variables as inputs to classify
electron-like tracks.
Track to calorimeter cluster position extrapolation
Although target tracks have very low pT,t h eb u l ki se x p e c t e dt or e a c ht h ec a l o r i m e t e ri n
principle. It is therefore plausible to also use the calorimeter information as inputs of the
LPET ID by extrapolating each track to its corresponding topo-cluster. The standard
extrapolation method of tracks to the calorimeter has been brieﬂy extended to support
the target pT range using a dedicated position matching method.
The basic idea of this method is to simply match the track to its corresponding cluster
based on its relative position coordinates  ⌘ ⌘ ⌘calo  ⌘track and   ⌘ calo  track;a
schematic diagram of the latter is shown in Fig. 5.2. Here, ( ⌘track, track)a n d( ⌘calo, calo)
correspond to the orientation of the particle at the interaction point and at the calorimeter,
respectively. Considering the barrel and endcap topology of the calorimeters and also the
curvature of electrons in 2 T magnetic ﬁeld, candidate clusters for each track are required
to pass the following loose position requirements:
• |⌘calo  ⌘track| < 0.1,
• cos(calo  track) > 0.8,
• | sin()ext| < 0.2, where
sin()ext ⌘ sin(calo  track)+0 .45 · trackCharge
trackPt [GeV] (if |⌘track| < 1.5),
sin()ext ⌘ sin(calo  track)+ 2
e1.13|⌘|e 1.13|⌘| · trackCharge
trackPt [GeV] (if |⌘track| > 1.5).
The ﬁrst requirement on  ⌘ simply requires the track and the calo cluster to be close to
each other, whereas the second and third requirements on   consider the electron track
curvature in the plane perpendicular to the beampipe.
Cluster selection using energy deposits

Input features
The list of the input variables used for the LPET ID are shown on Table 5.2.T h e i n p u t s
are roughly categorized into three groups: track, TRT, and calo inputs. Track inputs are
available for every track, whereas the TRT and calo inputs are available only when the
extension to the TRT and to the calorimeter is successfully performed, respectively.
The distributions of these variables have been checked to select which variables to use
as inputs to the LPET ID. It has been seen that the ﬁtting error variables of track inputs,
many of the TRT inputs, and the energy deposits in the inner calorimeter layers have high
importances for discriminating low- pT electron tracks against low- pT background tracks.
Fig. 5.6 shows the distributions of ﬁtting error variables of the track inputs. Due
to e↵ects of Bremsstrahlung, the ﬁtting errors of the track position tend to be larger
for electron tracks compared to background hadron tracks. It has also been seen that
this e↵ect is more apparent if tracks with the same “actual” (or truth) pT are compared;
electron tracks tend to have smaller reconstructed pT due to Bremsstrahlung, resulting in
larger values of ﬁtting errors.

Classiﬁcation with deep neural network
A deep neural network (DNN) algorithm has been developed to maximize the identiﬁca-
tion eciency of electron-like tracks by exploiting the track and its associated topo-cluster
variables. Fig. 5.9 shows the overall structure of the LPET ID DNN. The algorithm bi-
narily classiﬁes whether the input track is electron-like or non-electron-like; an output
electron likeliness score ranging from 0 to 1 is calculated based on the 79 input variables.
The TRT variables and calorimeter variables may not be available for some tracks due to
detector coverage and noise thresholds; in this case, these variables are set to 0. All of
the input values are standardized according to the transformation
z = x  µ
 , (5.1)
where x is the raw input value, µ is the average of that input in the training dataset, 
is the standard deviation of that input in the training dataset, and z is the standardized
input used for the DNN.
Figure 5.9: Overall structure of LPET ID DNN. 79 variables are used as inputs, and there are two hidden
layers with 128 nodes. The nodes are fully connected. The output electron likeliness score ranges from 0
to 1, with 1 meaning that the track is fully electron-like.
The training of LPET ID DNN has been done using MC “SingleParticleGun” electron
events, where there is only one electron per event at the stage of event generation. To
consider the e↵ects of pileup collisions on track reconstruction in the training, the events
were reconstructed by overlaying pileup collisions as described in Section 3.3.3.T h e s i g n a l
electron tracks used for the training were sampled from these events by dR matching
between the tracks and the truth electron particle. The background tracks used for the
training were also sampled from these events by vetoing the dR matched signal tracks.
For each signal and background tracks, ab out 4.5M samples were used for the training,
where 10% were assigned as validation samples. The training conﬁgurations of the neural
network; which have been optimized together with the model structure, are summarized
in Table 5.3.T h e a c c u r a c y a n d l o s s v a l u e s [165]d u r i n gt h et r a i n i n gp r o c e d u r ea r es h o w n

5.1.2 Optimization of selection criteria
Because the LPET ID was trained using SingleParticleGun samples, it is necessary to
adapt the LPET ID to the kinematics of the target signal. Therefore, optimized selection
criteria targeting electron tracks from ˜ 0
2 ! ˜0
1Z⇤ decay needs to be deﬁned. Since the
LPET ID DNN score may have some dependency on the track pT and ⌘, it is ideal to deﬁne
criteria depending on these three variables. This section describes the criteria deﬁnition
and the corresponding performance.
LPET ID WP DNN
To dynamically deﬁne selection criteria or “working point (WP)” dependent on the LPET
ID DNN score, track pT, and track ⌘, another DNN algorithm (hereinafter referred to as
LPET ID WP DNN) has been developed. Fig. 5.12 shows the overall structure of LPET
ID WP DNN. The WP DNN is designed to binarily classify whether the input track is
an electron coming from ˜0
2 ! ˜0
1Z⇤ decay or not; an output score is calculated based on
the three inputs. The inputs are standardized following Eq. ( 5.1).

⌘ and LPET ID DNN score. The structure is similar to that of LPET ID DNN, having fully connected

that the track is fully likely to be an electron from ˜ 0

5.1.3 Performance measurement with data and eciency cali-
bration
To apply the LPET ID WP in the analysis, its eciency must be calibrated with data.
This calibration has been done using Z ! ee events. The section brieﬂy summarizes
the calibration method and its results.
Ta rget event top ology
For the calibration of LPET ID, a well-known SM pro cess having a low- pT electron track
must be used as a control sample. Z ! ee events are introduced for this purpose,
considering its event topology with low background level. Fig. 5.16 shows a diagram
representing Z ! ee events. In this evaluation, only events with the following topology
is targeted; the electron closer to the photon is required to have very low pT, and the
other electron and the photon take away most of the energy.
Figure 5.16: Diagram representing Z ! ee events used for the calibration of LPET ID. The electron
closer to the photon is required to have very low pT for target events.
Event and track selection criteria
Events are selected using single electron trigger with trigger matching applied. Emiss
T <
50 GeV and b-tagged jet veto are required to suppress background events. A single
electron and a single photon are required for each event; the electron must pass the Tight
WP and have 30 <p T < 60 GeV, and the photon must pass the Tight WP and have
30 <p T < 55 GeV. Additional cuts on kinematic variables such as the electron-photon
invariant mass m(e, )a n d dR(e, )a r ei m p o s e dt os e l e c tZ ! ee -like events.
Target tracks are required to pass the TightPrimary WP (deﬁned in Section 4.1)a n d
have charges opposite to that of the electron. Usual pT and ⌘ requirements are applied:
0.5 <p T < 5G e Va n d |⌘| < 2.5. Cuts on kinematic variables such as the track-photon
invariant mass m(trk, ), dR(trk,e )a n d dR(trk, )a r ei m p o s e dt os u p p r e s sb a c k g r o u n d
tracks as much as possible.
More details of the event and track selection criteria are summarized in Appendix B.
All events and tracks passing these criteria are used for the LPET ID calibration.
78

=== PDF PAGE 90 ===
Strategy and method for performance measurement
The events and tracks passing the selection criteria above are dominated by background.
Z ! ee consists only about 10% of the events passing the selection criteria. The re-
maining background events are mostly Z ! ee;o n eo ft h ee l e c t r o n sf a k e sap h o t o n .
Tracks passing the selection criteria are dominated by hadronic tracks originating from
underlying events for both Z ! ee and Z ! ee events. These “fake” electron track con-
tributions can be inclusively considered as a single component. Therefore, the following
discussion categorizes the events into only two components: “true electron” tracks having
al o w -pT electron track which originate from Z ! ee and “fake electron” tracks which
do not.
To classify true and fake electron tracks in data, dR(trk, )ﬁ tm e t h o dh a sb e e ni n -
troduced. As shown in Fig. 5.17,t h es h a p e so fdR(trk, )d i s t r i b u t i o n sl a r g e l yd i ↵ e rf o r
true and fake electron tracks, because true electrons tend to be close to the photon, while
fake electrons, mostly hadrons from underlying events, are randomly positioned relative
to the selected photon for both Z ! ee and Z ! ee processes. The main strategy
of the calibration is to ﬁt these distributions for data using true and fake electron track
templates retrieved from MC. The ﬁt is performed independently for tracks passing and
failing the LPET ID WP, respectively. Fig. 5.18 shows the post-ﬁt distributions for tracks
passing and failing LPET ID WP 0.8.
Figure 5.17: Normalized distributions of dR(trk, ) for true and fake electron tracks. True electrons only
come from Z ! ee events, while fake electron tracks also come from Z ! ee events.

Eciency and scale factors
Fig. 5.19 shows the LPET ID WP eciency and scale factors derived by dR(trk, )ﬁ t
method. Large discrepancies between data and MC is observed, leading to small scale
factors. This is caused by poor MC modeling of input variables used for LPET ID DNN,
such as the TRT variables. By applying the scale factors to signal and background MC
tracks, these data/MC discrepancies of input variables are considered in an inclusive
manner. In addition, the scale factors for fake electron tracks, shown later in Appendix B
and not used in the analysis, have smaller values. Thus, the performance of LPET ID
WP for data is expected to be better than MC in terms of true eciency and fake rate.

5.2 Low-pT muon track
Similar to the low- pT electron track identiﬁcation in Section 5.1,an e wa l g o r i t h mh a sb e e n
developed for this search to identify low-pT muon tracks eluding the standard identiﬁcation
algorithm described in Section 4.6.T h e c o n t e n t s s u m m a r i z e d i n t h i s s e c t i o n a r e m a i n l y
from Ref. [ 166].
A muon loses about 3 GeV of its energy when traversing the ATLAS calorimeters as a
minimum ionizing particle (MIP) [ 163]. Thus, standard muon identiﬁcation described in
Section 4.6 has a lower pT threshold of 3 GeV as it requires hits in the muon spectrometers.
The basic idea of the new low- pT muon identiﬁcation algorithm, called the Low- pT Calo
Tag (LCaT) muon algorithm, is to identify low- pT muons that do not reach the muon
spectrometers by exploiting the di↵erence of energy deposits in the calorimeters. Such
muons tend to stop within the outer layers of the calorimeters and behave as MIPs in those
layers, leaving little energy. On the other hand, background hadrons such as pions induce
hadronic showers and produce topo-clusters with large energy deposits. Fig. 5.20 shows
the energy deposit distributions of muons and pions in the ﬁrst layer of Tile calorimeter.

single muon MCsingle pion MC
Figure 5.20: Energy deposit distributions for muons and pions in the ﬁrst layer of the Tile barrel [ 166].
Muons have a peak around 0.5 GeV corresponding to MIP energy deposit, while the energy of pions is
mostly distributed above that of a MIP.
The LCaT muon algorithm exploits track pT,t r a c k⌘,a n de n e r g yd e p o s i t si ne a c ho f
the layer of the topo-cluster by inputting them in a deep neural network. Thus, track and
topo-cluster matching is required, which has been performed by a method similar to that
described in Section 5.1.1.T h e n e u r a l n e t w o r k h a s b e e n t r a i n e d u s i n g s i g n a l m u o n s a n d
background particles in J/ ! µµ MC samples. The output muon likeliness score ranges
from 0 to 1 and is referred to as the LCaT DNN score.
A working point (WP) dedicated to the search for higgsinos has also been deﬁned by
an e u r a l - n e t w o r k - b a s e dm e t h o d ,s i m i l a rt ot h a tm e n t i o n e di nS e c .5.1.2.T h e L C a T m u o n

LCaT muon WP score larger than 0.6 is required in the analysis. Fig. 5.22 shows the

The calibration of the LCaT muon ID has been conducted by targeting the process of
J/ decay to two muons. In particular, tag and probe method was adopted to evaluate
the eciency of the LCaT muon ID for both data and MC. Target events are required
to pass the single muon trigger, and the trigger matched muon is considered as the tag
muon. The distribution of invariant mass of the tag muon and the probe muon is ﬁt using
crystal ball and quadratic functions to calculate the eciency of LCaT muon ID for probe
muons. The eciencies and scale factors obtained from this calibration method are shown
in Fig. 5.23.T h e s c a l e f a c t o r s a r e a p p l i e d i n t h i s s e a r c h t o c o r r e c t f o r t h e d i s c r e p a n c i e s
observed between data and MC, which are mostly within 10% for all pT and ⌘ regions.

6. Event Selection
To maximize sensitivity towards SUSY models having higgsinos with  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) ⇠
O(1) GeV, dedicated event selection strategies utilizing the low- pT lepton track taggers,
described in Sections 5.1 and 5.2,h a v eb e e nd e v e l o p e d . T h i ss e c t i o nd e s c r i b e st h eb a s i c
strategies of the analysis, preselection and variables used for the deﬁnition of the Sig-
nal Region, neural network used to deﬁne the ﬁnal discriminant, and the Signal Region
deﬁnition.
Two channels have been prepared for the analysis: electron channel and muon channel.
As the event selection strategies are similar for the two channels, they are described in a
parallel manner.

86

=== PDF PAGE 98 ===
The signatures of these target processes could be observed in multiple detectors. First,
with the requirement of R-parity conservation, ˜ 0
1 are weakly interacting stable particles
and are likely to traverse the ATLAS detector without leaving any signals. These are
indirectly observed as missing transverse momentum pmiss
T (deﬁned in Section 4.8), how-
ever, if two ˜0
1 are emitted back-to-back, the pmiss
T would cancel out and not be observed.
Therefore, an initial state radiation (ISR) jet is required in this analysis to boost the
target higgsino pair production system. In this case, the two ˜ 0
1 are emitted in the same
direction, opposite to the ISR jet, and could be observed as a high Emiss
T signature.
In addition, the features of the o↵-shell Z boson (Z⇤)h e l pi ne n h a n c i n gt h es e n s i t i v i t y .
The maximum value of the reconstructed Z⇤ mass equals the mass di↵erence  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1).
Moreover, since the system including the Z⇤ is boosted by the ISR jet, Z⇤ is emitted in
ad i r e c t i o nc l o s et ot h epmiss
T .
In previous studies [ 109, 167], the identiﬁcation eciencies of low- pT leptons were the
bottleneck of the search. In the target  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) of a few GeV, typical pT of the emitted
leptons are a few GeV, however, pT thresholds of standard lepton identiﬁcation are 4.5
GeV and 3 GeV for electrons and muons, respectively. This leads to very low acceptance
of the target signal processes, thus limiting the sensitivity.
To overcome this diculty, signal event eciency is recovered by applying the newly
developed low-pT lepton track taggers: LPET ID to the electron channel and LCaT muon
ID to the muon channel. In particular, these new taggers are applied to only one of
the two leptons from Z⇤; standard identiﬁcation is applied to the other lepton to realize
sucient background suppression. Hereinafter, the low- pT lepton identiﬁed with
the standard method is referred to as the “ ID lepton ”,w h i l ethe other low-
pT lepton identiﬁed with the newly developed method is referred to as the
“lepton track ”.
Furthermore, the kinematics of the two leptons from Z⇤ are exploited. The two leptons
are also boosted with the requirement of an ISR jet, which results in close-by ID lepton
and low- pT lepton track. The characteristics of the signal events are summarized in the
following list, and a schematic diagram of a typical signal event is shown in Fig. 6.2.
• Objects
– High Emiss
T
– High-pT ISR jet
– 1l o w -pT ID lepton
– 1l o w -pT lepton track
• Small angle between pmiss
T and Z⇤
• Small Z⇤ invariant mass
• Small angle between ID lepton and lepton track
The ﬂow of the analysis, described later in detail, is as follows. First, event preselection
is applied to select events with a high- pT hadronic jet, large missing transverse momentum,
and one low- pT ID lepton. Next, track selection is performed to select a single candidate
low-pT lepton track from many of the background hadronic tracks originating mainly from
underlying events. Finally, the newly developed low- pT lepton track identiﬁcation method
is applied to that candidate track to extract signal-like events.
87

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===
Figure 6.2: Schematic diagram of a signal event seen from the cross section of the ATLAS detector. An
ISR jet is required to boost the target higgsino pair production system. The Emiss
T and the two leptons
are objects originating from the boosted system.

nal Regions
Various requirements are imposed on distinctive variables of the event to discriminate
signals from background events and to deﬁne the Signal Regions (SRs).

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===
Emiss
T requirements
Signal events are expected to emit high Emiss
T due to two boosted ˜0
1.T h e r e f o r e , t h e
search uses data collected by inclusive Emiss
T triggers. The o✏ine Emiss
T is required
to be above 200 GeV, which ensures the trigger to be at its eciency plateau and
be fully ecient, as described in Section 3.2.
Leading jet selection
In each event, the reconstructed jet with the highest pT is identiﬁed as the leading
jet. Leading jet pT is required to be larger than 100 GeV to boost the higgsino
pair production system. In addition, the leading jet is required to pass the Tight
jet cleaning working point, aforementioned in Section 4.4,f o rt h er e d u c t i o no fn o n -
collision backgrounds.
Number of b-jets
B-veto is applied to suppress t¯t and single top backgrounds. It is required that
the number of b-jets with pT higher than 20 GeV be 0. The b-jets are identiﬁed
with DL1r algorithm with a condition corresponding to 85% eciency, as already
mentioned in Section 4.4.
Number of leptons
Exactly one lepton is required to target signal events; one electron for the electron
channel and one muon for the muon channel, where the preselection requirements
have been described in Sections 4.5 and 4.6,r e s p e c t i v e l y . T h i so n el e p t o nr e q u i r e -
ment assumes that the other lepton from Z⇤ decay fails the standard identiﬁcation
criteria, because it is expected to have very low pT.
ID lepton selection
In addition to the preselection requirements, further criteria are required for the ID
lepton to realize sucient background suppression. Both the electron and the muon
are required to pass the Medium working point and to be low- pT: pT < 10 GeV.
Promptness of ID lepton
For the target  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) of a few GeV, the lifetime of ˜ 0
2 is expected to be short
enough for the lepton to be prompt. Therefore, |z0 sin ✓| < 0.5m mi sr e q u i r e d
for both leptons, and for the transverse impact parameter, |d0/(d0)| < 5a n d
|d0/(d0)| < 3 are required for electrons and muons, respectively.
Isolation of ID lepton
Signal leptons originating from Z⇤ are expected to have little surrounding activity
compared to background leptons. Thus, the following “isolation” requirements are
imposed. Within a cone of dR =0 .3, no charged tracks with pT > 1G e Va s s o c i a t e d
to the hard-scatter vertex (excluding the track of the lepton itself) are allowed.
However, if the later mentioned low- pT lepton track candidate is within the cone,
its contribution is subtracted to avoid vetoing signal tracks.
6.2.2 Variables used for low- pT lepton track preselection
Candidate tracks, which the newly developed low- pT lepton track ID will be applied to,
are preselected by the following criteria. Events with no candidate tracks fail the event

are also used to preselect candidate tracks. The d0 and z0 of the lepton and the

6.3 Parameterized neural network for event selection
The event topology of signal events has large dependence on the mass splitting of the
higgsinos, namely  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1). For very small  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1), the leptons emitted from ˜0
2 !
˜0
1Z⇤ decay have very low pT,a n dt h et w o ˜0
1 tend to become more collimated, leading
to higher Emiss
T values. In this case, variables such as dR(lep, trk) and  (pmiss
T ,Z ⇤)h a v e
very small values. For larger  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1), the leptons have higher pT, leading to lower
Emiss
T ,a n dt h e r e f o r e ,l a r g e rdR(lep, trk) and  (pmiss
T ,Z ⇤).
To consider these dep endencies and to classify signal and background events in a dy-
namic manner, parameterized neural network (pNN) [ 111] is introduced. Fig. 6.3 shows
a schematic diagram of pNN. By choosing  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1)a st h ep h y s i c sp a r a m e t e r✓ (here-
inafter referred to as pNN- ✓), it is possible to realize  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1)-dependent event selection
with a single network model. This also simpliﬁes the training procedure, as pNN has the
ability to smoothly interpolate between  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1)u s e df o rt r a i n i n g ,t h u sg i v i n gt h ep o s -
sibility to explore various mass splitting scenarios with limited number of training samples.
This section describes the inputs, the model and its training, and the performance of the
pNN designed for event selection.

Fig. 1 Left,i n d i v i d u a ln e t w o r k sw i t hi n p u tf e a t u r e s (x1,x2),e a c h
trained with examples with a single value of some parameter θ=θa,θb.
The individual networks are purely functions of the input features. Per-
formance for intermediate values of θis not optimal nor does it nec-
essarily vary smoothly between the networks. Right , a single network
trained with input features (x1,x2)as well as an input parameter θ; such
a network is trained with examples at several values of the parameter θ
tion of ¯θintroduces additional considerations in the training
procedure. While traditionally the training only requires the
conditional distribution of ¯xgiven ¯θ(which is predicted by
the theory and detector simulation), now the training data
has some implicit prior distribution over ¯θas well (which is
arbitrary). When the network is used in practice it will be
to predict yconditional on both ¯xand ¯θ, so the distribution
of¯θused for training is only relevant in how it affects the
quality of the resulting parameterized network – it does not
imply that the resulting inference is Bayesian. In the studies
presented below, we simply use equal sized samples for a few
discrete values of ¯θ.A n o t h e ri s s u ei st h a ts o m eo ra l lo ft h e
components of ¯θmay not be meaningful for a particular target
class. For instance, the mass of a new particle is not meaning-
ful for the background training examples. In what follows,
we randomly assign values to those components of ¯θaccord-
ing to the same distribution used for the signal class. In the
examples studied below, the networks have enough general-
ization capacity and the training sets are large enough that
the resulting parameterized classiﬁer performs well without
any tuning of the training procedure. However, the robust-
ness of the resulting parameterized classiﬁer to the implicit
distribution of ¯θin the training sample will in general depend
on the generalization capacity of the classiﬁer, the number of
training examples, the physics encoded in the distributions
p(¯x|¯θ,y), and how much those distributions change with ¯θ.

6.3.4 Application
In the case where a simple neural network is used for a search analysis, the neural network
model is trained and optimized for each mass point. On the other hand, pNN has a single
model trained to perform for each of the mass points, but with additional degrees of
freedom, which could be adjusted manually. In the analysis, the degree of freedom is the
pNN-✓,c o r r e s p o n d i n gt ot h em(˜0
2, ˜0
1).
The pNN in this search analysis is trained with pNN- ✓ = 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4 and 5 GeV. The
same values are used for the search by ﬁxing the pNN- ✓ to each value. Therefore, there
are six models to choose from for each mass point. Straightforwardly, the best performing
model for each mass point should be the one having the same pNN- ✓,h o w e v e r ,t h i sm i g h t
not always be the case due to ﬂuctuations. Hence, the best performing pNN- ✓ is chosen
for each mass point by comparing the ﬁnal CLs values obtained from statistical analysis
described later in Section 9.
6.4 Signal Region deﬁnition
Fig. 6.10 shows the ﬂow of this search analysis. Event and track preselection requirements
deﬁned in Section 6.2 are ﬁrst applied. Next, a single track with the highest pNN score
is selected for each event. This is performed by reconstructing the Z⇤ candidate and
applying the pNN with each of the preselected tracks. After the track selection, cuts on
(pmiss
T ,Z ⇤) and the pNN score are applied to select signal-like events. The regions that
fail these requirements are used as Validation Regions (VRs) of the background estimation
method. Finally, low- pT lepton track ID is applied to the selected track with dedicated
WPs. The regions passing the low- pT lepton track ID requirement are used as Signal
Regions (SRs). The deﬁnition of SRs is discussed in this section.
Figure 6.10: Diagram of the overall analysis ﬂow. The basic ﬂow is common to the electron and muon
channels, while the detailed requirements slightly di↵er for each channel.
98

=== PDF PAGE 110 ===
The general idea of SR deﬁnition is to maximize the sensitivity towards signals by
choosing regions where the signal contribution is relatively high compared to SM back-
ground contributions. This is achieved by applying two independent selections on the
main variables of the analysis: the pNN score and the low- pT lepton track ID score. By
ﬁrst selecting events with high pNN scores, background events having non-signal-like event
topology are vetoed. Then by selecting events having a track with high low- pT lepton
track ID WP score, signiﬁcant background suppression is achieved by rejecting hadronic
and fake tracks, which are the main background track components in the targeted regime.
Table 6.5 summarizes the selection criteria of SRs for the electron channel and the
muon channel. The requirement on  (pmiss
T ,Z ⇤) is imposed to keep the orthogonality
with Validation Regions deﬁned for the background estimation method and is common to
both channels. The requirements on the pNN score and low- pT lepton track ID WP score
have been optimized to realize maximum sensitivity with expected background assuming
20% uncertainty.
Table 6.5: Summary of the selection criteria of SR.
Variable Electron channel Muon channel
(pmiss
T ,Z ⇤) < 1
pNN score > 0.95
Low-pT lepton track ID LPET WP score > 0.8 LCaT muon WP score > 0.6
99

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
7. Methodology of Background Esti-
mation
In this section, the main sources of background events are presented, followed by the
details of the strategy of estimating the background contribution in the Signal Region.
7.1 Background components
All SM processes with a high- pT jet and a boosted system with neutrinos in the ﬁnal
state could pass the leading jet and Emiss
T selection of this search. The requirements
for the ID lepton, which the standard lepton identiﬁcation is applied to, are relatively
tight, hence the ID lepton is expected to be a real lepton in most cases. On the other
hand, the requirements for the low- pT lepton track, which the newly developed low- pT
lepton track identiﬁcation method is applied to, are much looser; its main contribution is
hadronic/fake tracks. Since the tracks are required to be associated with the hard-scatter
vertex and have no surrounding activities, most of the background tracks are expected to
be originating from underlying events. Some of these tracks could potentially imitate the
signal lepton track originating from Z⇤.
Considering that the target event topology requires a high- pT hadronic jet, large miss-
ing momentum, and a single lepton, the most probable background process is W +jets,
where the W decays leptonically. Fig. 7.1 shows the diagram of W +jets process. In this
process, the true lepton originating from the W will likely be identiﬁed as the ID lepton,
and the neutrino will likely result in high Emiss
T signature. As already mentioned, the
hadronic/fake tracks would fake the low- pT lepton track in this case. Moreover, there
could also be small contributions from true leptonic tracks. Z+jets, diboson, and other
processes which could possibly have two or more true leptons in their ﬁnal states, could
contribute as “true lepton” backgrounds. The dominant background with a “fake low- pT
lepton track” is estimated by a data-driven method described in Section 7.2, whereas the
subdominant background with a “true low- pT lepton track” is estimated directly from
MC simulation as described in Section 7.3. A semi data-driven method is therefore used
for the background estimation of the analysis.
100

=== PDF PAGE 112 ===
Figure 7.1: Diagram of W +jets process. This process is the main background of this search.
7.2 Data-driven estimation of 1L + fake lepton track
background
Ad a t a - d r i v e nm e t h o dh a sb e e na d o p t e dt oe s t i m a t et h ed o m i n a n th a d r o n i c / f a k et r a c k
background in the SR due to the following reasons:
• The reliability of MC simulation modeling of low- pT hadronic/fake tracks is not
fully understood,
• Systematic uncertainties for the MC modeling of fake low- pT lepton track would not
have to be considered, allowing for a more accurate estimation.
The general idea of this method is to measure and apply the pass-to-fail ratios or the
“transfer factors” of the low- pT lepton track ID in certain Control Regions (CRs), having
di↵erent kinematics compared to the SR. The validity of the method is veriﬁed in multiple
Validation Regions (VRs). This transfer factor method is compatible with the analysis
ﬂow visualized in Fig. 6.10 and is common to both electron and muon channels.
Although the method mentioned above is expected to estimate nearly 90% of the total
background in the SR, it has been observed in VRs that the above method does not fully
consider relatively small contributions from true leptons to the low- pT lepton track. These
contributions are estimated using MC simulations as discussed later in Section 7.3.F o r
the estimation of fake lepton track background, the contributions from “true leptons” are
subtracted from data in each of the CRs to properly measure the hadronic/fake track
transfer factors.
101

=== PDF PAGE 113 ===
CR deﬁnitions and estimation procedures
Here, the deﬁnitions of the CRs used for measuring and applying the low- pT lepton track
ID transfer factors are described. The deﬁnitions are identical for both electron and muon
channels.
Figure 7.2: Kinematic plane diagram used for region deﬁnitions. In terms of kinematics, the SR, CRs,
and VRs are deﬁned by imposing cuts on the pNN score and  (pmiss
T ,Z ⇤).
Fig. 7.2 shows the kinematic plane used to deﬁne the SR, CRs, and VRs. The SR,
which has already been deﬁned in Section 6.4,i sw i t h i nt h e↵ -region, where pNN score
threshold p is deﬁned in Table 6.5 for each channel. It must be noted that the kinematic
regions deﬁned here do not consider the low- pT lepton track ID requirements in Table 6.5;
therefore, the SR is deﬁned by the region passing the low- pT lepton track ID WP within
the ↵ -region.
Since the low-pT lepton track ID and the event selection pNN are deﬁned independently
of each other, the low pNN score region, dominated by SM background, is a valid region
for measuring the transfer factors. In particular, the kinematic -region, with pNN scores
lower than 0.4, is used. Within this -region, the region passing the low- pT lepton track
ID WP requirements (WP pass region) in Table 6.5 is deﬁned as CR-A, and the region
failing the WP requirements (WP fail region) is deﬁned as CR-B. The transfer factors are
then measured by taking the ratio of the number of events belonging to CR-A to that of
CR-B. To consider the e↵ects of indirect relations between the low- pT lepton track ID and
the pNN through the track kinematics, the transfer factors are measured separately for
each of the track pT bins listed in Table 7.1.T h i s t r a c kpT binned transfer factor method
is capable of estimating the pNN score shape in the WP pass region.
The measured transfer factors are then applied to the WP fail region within the
kinematic ↵ -region, deﬁned as CR-C. This method is expected to correctly estimate the
pNN score shape in the SR, and its validity has been checked with MC simulated samples.
Fig. 7.3 shows a schematic diagram of the background estimation method, summarizing
the deﬁnition of each CR.

are binned by the track pT to correctly estimate the pNN score shape in the SR. The measured transfer
factors are then applied to CR-C to estimate the background in the SR.

7.3 MC estimation of 1L + true lepton track back-
ground
As brieﬂy mentioned in the previous section, the subdominant contribution of 1L +
true lepton track background is estimated using MC simulation. These contributions
are considered by adding them directly to the data-driven estimated fake lepton track
contribution in the SRs and VRs. The considered background MC samples are listed
in Table 3.3, and true lepton tracks are selected by matching with corresponding truth
particles. Processes which have two or more prompt leptons, such as Z+jets and diboson
production, tend to have small but non-negligible contributions. The e↵ects of non-
prompt leptons from processes such as dijet production and +jets have also been checked
but were found to be negligible.
7.4 Validation strategy
The background estimation method described in Sections 7.2 and 7.3 must be validated
by checking the agreement of estimated background and data in certain VRs. This is
done in multiple VRs, having suciently low signal contamination. The VRs are used to
evaluate the non-closure systematics of the background estimation method later described
in Section 8.
Since the estimation method assumes that the transfer factors measured in the low
pNN score region are also valid in the high pNN score region, this validity must be
checked with a dedicated VR. This is done within the kinematic -region in Fig. 7.2,
deﬁned by pNN score > 0.4a n d (pmiss
T ,Z ⇤) > 1.4. Although the SR is deﬁned by
(pmiss
T ,Z ⇤) < 1.0, the cut threshold for the -region has been raised to 1.4 to suppress
signal contamination. Fig. 7.4 shows the schematic diagram of the validation using the
kinematic -region. By applying the measured transfer factors to CR-highLarge, which
corresponds to the WP fail region within the -region, the number of events in VR-
highLarge, deﬁned by WP pass region within the -region, is estimated.
Additional VRs are considered to verify the validity of the background estimation
method in the ↵ -region. This is performed by ﬂipping the charge or the ﬂavor relations
between the ID lepton and the low- pT lepton track. For the SR shown in Fig. 7.3,t h e
ID lepton and the low- pT lepton track are required to have opposite signs (OS) and be of
the same ﬂavor (SF), namely, electrons for the electron channel and muons for the muon
channel. For additional VRs, the ID lepton and the low- pT lepton track are requied to
have the same sign (SS) or di↵erent ﬂavors (DF), with other requirements set identically
to those of the SR. For these two VRs and their corresponding CRs, the pNN score
thresholds for ↵ -regions are lowered to 0.4 to increase statistics.
For the same sign VR, deﬁned as VR-SS, the transfer factors are measured and applied
within the same sign region; therefore, additional CRs named CR-SS-A, CR-SS-B, and
CR-SS-C are deﬁned in correspondence with the three CRs shown in Fig. 7.3. Fig. 7.5
shows the schematic diagram of the validation using VR-SS. The estimated background
in VR-SS is compared to data for validation.
For the di↵erent ﬂavor VR, deﬁned as VR-DF, the transfer factors are measured and
applied within the di↵erent ﬂavor region. For the electron channel, an ID muon and a low-
104

=== PDF PAGE 116 ===

factors measured in the low pNN score region are applied to CR-highLarge to estimate the number of
events in VR-highLarge.
Figure 7.5: Schematic diagram of the background estimation validation in VR-SS. CR-SS-A and CR-SS-

transfer factors are binned by the track pT using the same binning as the OS-SF regions. The measured
transfer factors are then applied to CR-SS-C to estimate the background in VR-SS.

pT electron track are considered to measure the transfer factors of LPET ID. For the muon

CR-DF-C are deﬁned in correspondence with the three CRs shown in Fig. 7.3. Fig. 7.6
shows the schematic diagram of the validation using VR-DF. The estimated background
in VR-DF is compared to data for validation.
Figure 7.6: Schematic diagram of the background estimation validation in VR-DF. CR-DF-A and CR-
DF-B are used to measured the di↵erent ﬂavor transfer factors (TF-DF) of the low- pT lepton track ID
WP. The transfer factors are binned by the track pT using the same binning as the OS-SF region. The
measured transfer factors are then applied to CR-DF-C to estimate the background in VR-DF.
7.5 Summary of CR and VR deﬁnitions
In summary, there are three VRs and 10 CRs for each channel as listed in Table 7.2.
Three CRs (CR-A/B/C) are necessary for estimating the SR background: two for TF
measurement and one for TF application. The remaining seven CRs are dedicated to
validation: one (CR-highLarge) for VR-highLarge, three (CR-A/B/C-SS) for VR-SS, and
three (CR-A/B/C-DF) for VR-DF. It should be again noted that the contributions from
true lepton tracks are subtracted from CRs before measuring the transfer factors and are
added to SRs and VRs afterwards.

8. Systematic Uncertainties
This section describes the systematic uncertainties considered in this search: experimen-
tal uncertainties, theoretical uncertainties, and analysis speciﬁc uncertainties. As the
dominant background is the fake lepton track contribution estimated by the data-driven
method, the non-closure uncertainty on that component is the most dominant source of
the systematic uncertainty. Although contributions of background MC samples are rel-
atively small and their systematic uncertainties should have little impact on the results,
they have been evaluated to a certain extent.
8.1 Experimental uncertainties
Experimental uncertainties are considered for the MC samples to account for the mis-
modeling in detector responses and imperfections in calibrations. The considered exper-
imental uncertainties are described below. Experimental uncertainties for the signal MC
samples have been implemented straightforwardly by handling each of the uncertainties
as separate nuisance parameters in the statistical analysis framework later described in
Section 9.O n t h e o t h e r h a n d , f o r b a c k g r o u n d M C s a m p l e s u s e d f o r e s t i m a t i n g t h e t r u e
lepton contribution, the experimental uncertainties have been handled as a ﬂat 50% en-
velope systematic of all uncertainties described below and implemented to the statistical
framework as a single nuisance parameter. This is to prevent ﬁt instabilities in the frame-
work, caused by low MC statistics. As the true lepton track contribution accounts for
only about 10% of the SR background, its uncertainties are of second order and have
minimal impact on the total uncertainty.
Electrons
• Energy resolution: up/down variations in the resolution of the electron energy.
• Energy scale: up/down variations in the scale of the electron energy.
• Reconstruction eciency: up/down variations in the error of the electron recon-
struction scale factors.
• Identiﬁcation eciency: up/down variations in the error of the electron identiﬁca-
tion working-point scale factors.
• Isolation eciency: up/down variations in the error of the electron isolation working-
point scale factors.
109

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===
Muons
• Inner Detector smearing: up/down variations in the smearing of the muon tracks
in the Inner Detector.
• Muon spectrometer smearing: up/down variations in the smearing of the muon
tracks in the muon spectrometers.
• Momentum scale: up/down variations in the muon momentum scale.
• Sagitta resolution: up/down charge-dependent variations in the scale of the momen-
tum and its statistical error associated to the charge-dependent corrections.
• Reconstruction eciency: up/down variations in the statistical and systematic er-
rors on the muon reconstruction scale factors.
• Isolation eciency: up/down variations in statistical and systematic errors on the
muon isolation working-point scale factors.
• Track-to-vertex association (TTV A) eciency: up/down variations in statistical and
systematic errors on the muon TTV A scale factors.
Jets
• Jet energy resolution: up/down variations in the smearing of the MC jets to the
data to correct the MC resolution. These uncertainties are evaluated from dijet
events and zero bias data using random cones.
• Jet energy scale: up/down variations in the scale of the jet momentum. These uncer-
tainties are derived from the dijet eta-intercalibration across eta, Z(! ee, µµ)+jets,
+jets and multijet balance, and the propagation of single particles in high- pT
regimes.
• Jet vertex tagger: up/down variations accounting for the residual contamination
from pileup jets in the central region.
• Flavor tagging eciency: up/down variations in the error on the scale factors used
to correct the simulation-over-data ﬂavor tagging rate.
Missing transverse momentum
• Soft term resolution: one-sided variations of the Emiss
T track soft term resolution,
parallel and perpendicular to the hard term.
• Soft term scale: up/down variations in the Emiss
T track soft term scale, obtained by
scaling the soft term magnitude in the direction of phard
T .
110

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
Others
• Pileup: up/down variations of the pileup weight used to reweight nominal MC
predictions to reproduce the correct distribution of the number of pp collisions per
bunch crossing in data.
• Track impact parameter resolution: one-sided variations on the transverse and lon-
gitudinal impact parameter resolutions to account for any di↵erences with respect to
data. These uncertainties are obtained by replacing the nominal impact parameter
with a random value taken from a Gaussian distribution, whose central value is the
original MC impact parameter and whose sigma is taken from dedicated smearing
maps from studies on MC/data resolution di↵erences.
8.2 Theoretical uncertainties
Theoretical uncertainties for signal and background MC samples have been evaluated
to consider generator-level modeling uncertainties. Note that for the background MC
samples, all theoretical uncertainties have been considered only for diboson and V +jets
samples, which are the dominant components of the true lepton track contribution. Un-
certainties of other background processes have negligible impact and are hence not con-
sidered.
The following variations of QCD renormalization and factorization scales and parton
distribution functions (PDFs) have been taken into account for both signal and back-
ground MC samples. These uncertainties are combined by taking their quadratic sum.
QCD renormalization and factorization scales
Renormalization and factorization scales ( µR and µF )a r eu s e dt oe s t i m a t et h eu n -
certainties originating from truncated higher orders in the perturbative expansion
of the partonic cross-section corrections. The uncertainties are estimated by varying
µR and µF by a factor of two resulting in 7-point variations of {µR,µ F } = {0.5, 0.5},
{1.0, 0.5}, {0.5, 1.0}, {1.0, 1.0}, {2.0, 1.0}, {1.0, 2.0}, {2.0, 2.0}.3 - p o i n t v a r i a t i o n s
of {µR,µ F } = {0.5, 0.5}, {1.0, 1.0}, {2.0, 2.0} are used when the 7-point variations
are unavailable at generator-level. The envelope of the 7-point (3-point) variations
are taken as the overall uncertainty.
Parton Distribution Function (PDF)
PDFs at a given scale ( Q) are determined by ﬁtting a given functional form to
data. The PDF at di↵erent energy scales in the MC generator is estimated by
DGLAP equations [ 168–170]. Therefore, experimental uncertainties in the used
dataset and uncertainty on the functional form for PDF ﬁts are sources of uncer-
tainties. Truncated higher order calculations of DGLAP equations could also be
sources of uncertainties, however, they are considered by varying the renormaliza-
tion and factorization scales as mentioned above.
Additionally, the following uncertainties are considered, depending on the signal and
background processes.
111

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
Cross section uncertainty (for signal MC only)
The theoretical uncertainty on the cross section for signal MC is considered. Similar
to the nominal cross section value, its uncertainty is also evaluated to NLO+NLL
accuracy using Resummino [130–135]. The relative uncertainty is less than 5% for
all mass points.
Strong coupling constant ( ↵ S ) (for background MC only)
↵ S is determined experimentally and quoted at the Z mass scale ( Q = mZ ). ↵ S
at di↵erent energy scales in the MC generators are predicted by renormalization
group equations (RGEs), although the calculations are truncated at a given order.
Therefore, experimental errors of ↵ S and missing higher order calculations of RGEs
need to be accounted as uncertainties of ↵ S .P D F s e t s w i t h d i ↵ e r e n t↵ S values are
used to estimate the uncertainties.
8.3 Analysis speciﬁc uncertainties
Additional uncertainties considered for this analysis are described below.
Low-pT lepton track ID scale factor uncertainty
As described in Sections 5.1 and 5.2,l o w -pT lepton track ID scale factors are ap-
plied to MC samples to consider the behavior di↵erences between data and MC
measured by the calibration. Up/down variations are applied by accounting for
the uncertainties originating from the calibration, which primarily depends on the
statistical uncertainties of the calibration data and the parametrization by track
pT. Uncertainties depending on track ⌘ and other parameters have been omitted
for simplicity, because the impact of this scale factor uncertainty on the total back-
ground estimation is small, as this uncertainty is only applied to the subdominant
true lepton track background component.
Non-closure uncertainty for background estimation
The non-closure of the background estimation method is expected to originate
mainly from the uncertainties in the extrapolation of pNN scores using track pT
binned transfer factors, as the transfer factors could have dependecies on other vari-
ables. These uncertainties are evaluated by comparing the data and estimation in
VRs and are applied as ﬂat systematic uncertainties to the fake lepton track com-
ponents in the SR. As a result of the evaluation, 15% and 40% ﬂat systematics are
applied to electron and muon channels, respectively.
CR statistical uncertainty
The statistical uncertainties of data in the CRs used for estimating the fake lep-
ton background in the SR also have some impact. These have been considered
as normalization factor and transfer factor uncertainties in the statistical analysis
framework, later described in Section 9.
MC statistical uncertainty
The statistical uncertainties of the signal and background MC samples have been
considered in all regions. The impact of the statistical uncertainties of the back-
ground MC samples is minimal, since the CRs are dominated by fake lepton contri-
bution, and only a fraction of events are truly leptonic in the SR.
112

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===

as the transfer factor and normalization factor uncertainties, have non-negligible e↵ects.
On the other hand, as the contribution from true lepton track component is small, corre-
sponding uncertainties such as the 50% envelope experimental uncertainty, MC statistical
uncertainty, and the low- pT lepton track ID scale factor uncertainty, have relatively small
impact on the estimated background.

9. Statistical Analysis
Statistical ﬁts have been performed to estimate the number of events in the SRs and test
its compatibility under various hypotheses. This has been done with a proﬁle likelihood
ratio approach using the HistFitter package [171]. This chapter describes the deﬁnition
of the likelihood function and the methodology of hypothesis testing.
9.1 Likelihood function
The likelihood L of the electron or muon channel analysis is deﬁned as the product
of Poisson distributions of the number of events in the SR and CRs and the Gaussian
probability densities of the systematic uncertainties:
L(n|µsig, s, b, ✓)= PSR ⇥ PCR-A ⇥ PCR-B ⇥ PCR-C ⇥ Csyst, (9.1)
where Pi is the Poisson measurement in region i,a n d Csyst is the product of Gaussian
probability distributions of all considered systematic uncertainties. Poisson measurement
Pi is represented by the observed number of events ni and the expected number of i in
region i:
Pi = P (ni|i)= ni

9.3 Fit conﬁguration
All of the systematic uncertainties described in Section 8 are implemented in the statis-
tical ﬁt framework as nuisance parameters. To speed up the ﬁtting procedure, nuisance
parameters are pruned when their deviation from the nominal value is less than 1%.
Simultaneous ﬁt for data-driven estimation of fake lepton tracks
The dominant fake lepton track background is estimated using a data-driven method by
measuring and applying transfer factors in dedicated CRs, as described in Section 7.2.
Separate CRs are prepared for each track pT bin shown in Table 7.1 to perform the
track pT binned transfer factor method. The SRs and VRs are prepared as track pT
inclusive regions; the track pT binned estimations are summed in these regions. All of the
procedures are implemented in the statistical ﬁt framework, and the transfer factors and
the expected number of fake lepton background are obtained by the simultaneous ﬁt.
117

=== PDF PAGE 129 ===
The transfer factor method is performed by ﬁrst injecting dummy events into each CR
and normalizing its number to the number of events in data subtracted by the true lepton
contribution estimated by MC. Therefore, the normalization factor µCR is calculated by
µCR · ndummy = ndata  ntrueLep, (9.14)
where n represents the number of events. ndummy represents the initial value used for the
ﬁt and does not have any meanings by itself; its value multiplied by µCR represents the fake
lepton track yield in the CR. The transfer factors are then calculated by taking the ratio
of normalization factors of CR-A and CR-B as illustrated in Fig. 7.3. When the number of
dummy events are set equally for CR-A and CR-B, the ratio of the normalization factors
equals to the actual ratio of the number of events, hence the transfer factor:

nCR-C
data  nCR-C
trueLep

· TF = µCR-C · nCR-C
dummy · TF. (9.16)
Finally, the total estimated background events in SR nSR
totalEst is obtained by adding the fake
lepton component estimated by the data-driven method and the true lepton component
estimated by MC simulation:
nSR
totalEst = nSR
fakeLep + nSR
trueLep. (9.17)
In the ﬁt, µCR-A,T F= µCR-A/µCR-B and µCR-C are the ﬁt parameters and thus, the transfer
factors are obtained within the ﬁt framework. The same procedures are performed for all
VRs, following the diagrams illustrated in Fig. 7.4–7.6.

10. Results
This section shows and discusses the results of the statistical analysis, which has been
described in Section 9.
10.1 Background-only ﬁt
Fits have been performed with the background-only hypothesis to obtain the expected
number of SM background events in the SRs. The method has been ﬁrst validated with
dedicated VRs as described in Section 7.4.I t m u s t b e n o t e d t h a t t h e ﬁ t h a s b e e n p e r -
formed for each of the six pNN- ✓ values for both electron and muon channels. Moreover,
the relative uncertainty on the expected total background is larger for the muon chan-
nel, as the dominant non-closure uncertainty assigned to the muon channel is larger as
mentioned in Section 8.3.
Fig. 10.1 and Fig. 10.2 show the agreement between observed data and estimated back-
ground yields in the VRs for each pNN- ✓, for the electron channel and the muon channel,
respectively. The deviation between the observed data and the estimated background has
been quantiﬁed by the signiﬁcance Z [174]d e ﬁ n e da s

10.2 Model-dependent ﬁt
Since no signiﬁcant excess has been observed in all SRs, model-dependent ﬁts have been
performed to set exclusion limits on the target signal models, described in Sections 1.3.1
and 3.3.1.T h e ﬁ t s h a v e b e e n p e r f o r m e d f o r b o t h e l e c t r o n a n d m u o n c h a n n e l s w i t h a l l
six pNN- ✓s: 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, and 5 GeV. Fig. 10.7 and Fig. 10.8 show the observed and
expected exclusion limits for each pNN- ✓ for electron and muon channels, respectively.
All of the exclusion limits are at 95% conﬁdence level and are shown in the m(˜±
1 )=
1
2

m(˜0
2)+ m(˜0
1)
 
vs  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) plane for easy comparison with results from other
searches.
Moreover, the six pNN- ✓sh a v eb e e nc o m b i n e dt od r a was i n g l ee x c l u s i o nc o n t o u rf o r
each channel. As these SRs could have possible overlaps, the envelope of the six contours
has been taken by adopting the pNN- ✓ with the smallest expected CLs value for each
of the signal mass points. Fig. 10.9 shows the observed and expected exclusion limits
obtained by combining the six pNN- ✓sf o re a c hc h a n n e l . F o rt h ee l e c t r o nc h a n n e l ,s l i g h t
excess in data for pNN- ✓ = 2 GeV and slight deﬁcit in data for pNN- ✓ =4G e Vh a v e
resulted in a rather distorted observed limit with respect to the expected limit. For the
muon channel, slight excess in data for pNN- ✓ = 4 GeV and pNN- ✓ =5G e Vh a v er e s u l t e d
in a looser limit for higher mass splitting values.
Up to this point, the electron and muon channels have been analyzed and evaluated
separately, however, as these channels are orthogonal to each other, they can be statis-
tically combined by performing channel inclusive ﬁts. Channel inclusive ﬁts have been
performed for the six pNN- ✓ conﬁgurations, and the conﬁgurations have been combined
by the above method. Fig. 10.10 shows the observed and expected exclusion limits for
each pNN- ✓ for the channel inclusive ﬁts, and Fig. 10.11 shows the pNN combined ob-
served and expected exclusion limits of the channel inclusive ﬁts. The observed limit and
the exclusion limit is similar for the channel inclusive ﬁt due to coincidental e↵ects; the
e↵ect of excess observed in the higher mass splitting values for the muon channel has
been complemented by the e↵ect of deﬁcit observed in similar regions for the electron
channel. The ﬁnal observed exclusion limit, obtained from the statistical combination of
both electron and muon channels and the combination of six pNN- ✓s, extends beyond
m(˜±
1 )=1 3 0G e Vf o rc o m p r e s s e dm a s sr e g i o n sw i t hm(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) = 1–2 GeV, which have
never been explored by experiments at the LHC.
Fig. 10.12 shows the pNN combined observed and expected exclusion limits overlaid
on results observed by existing analyses. It can be seen that the search presented in this
dissertation plays a crucial role of searching the gap between the previous soft 2L search
and the displaced track search.

10.3 Model-independent ﬁt
Model-independent ﬁts have been performed to set upper limits on the number of BSM
events in the target search regions, having an event topology with a high- pT hadronic jet,
large missing transverse momentum, a low- pT lepton, and a very-low- pT lepton-like track.
For this purp ose, as summarized in Table 10.13,t h r e ed e d i c a t e d“ d i s c o v e r y ”S R sh a v e
been deﬁned by combining the electron and muon channel SRs for three pNN- ✓ values:
pNN-✓ =1 , 3, and 5 GeV, corresponding to small- m,m i d - m and large- m.E a c h o f
them has been named as SR-small-dM, SR-mid-dM, and SR-large-dM.
Table 10.13: Summary of the discovery SRs. Three regions are deﬁned by combining the electron and
muon channel exclusion SRs for three pNN- ✓ values.
SR-small-dM SR-mid-dM SR-large-dM
pNN-✓ [GeV] 1 3 5
Fig. 10.13 shows the p-values as a function of signal strength µsig for each of the
discovery SRs, obtained from the model-independent upper limit scans. Table 10.14
summarizes the model-independent upper limits obtained from these scans. For SR-
small-dM, SR-mid-dM, and SR-large-dM, the observed upper limits on the cross section
of BSM processes are 0.05 fb, 0.13 fb, and 0.14 fb, respectively.

11. Discussion
11.1 Slight excess observed for the muon channel
Although there was no signiﬁcant excesses of observed data in any of the SRs, it can be
seen in Fig. 10.4 that a slight excess of 1 .5 has been observed in the SRs of the muon
channel for pNN- ✓ = 5 GeV. It is dicult to determine the source of this excess without
additional statistics, however, some candidates of the source is discussed here.
Fig. 11.1 shows some of the important kinematic distributions of the observed data
and estimated background in the SR for the muon channel for pNN- ✓ = 5 GeV. It can
be seen from these distributions that the excess comes from the high track pT region, in
particular, the region with pT = 3–5 GeV. Dependencies on other variables have not been
observed.
If the excess is from an actual BSM process, it is less-likely that it is from the targeted
higgsino pair production signal. This is because, the mass region which pNN- ✓ =5G e V
targets has large overlaps with the region which has already been excluded by the soft
2L search. Moreover, the electron channel does not present a similar excess; this is also
incompatible with the higgsino signal hypothesis. Therefore, if one assumes that this
excess is from BSM, it would likely be from another process involving the high- pT jet
+ Emiss
T topology, favoring the emission of low- pT muons over low- pT electrons. On the
other hand, if the excess is from SM background, it is likely that the excess is from
the dominant W (! µ⌫)+jets background, in which a hadronic track from an underlying
event is accidentally identiﬁed as a low- pT muon track. However, hadronic tracks from
underlying events tend to have lower pT,s oi ti sn o tf u l l yc l e a rw h yt h ee x c e s sg a t h e r si n
the high track pT regions. As mentioned earlier, more data would be necessary to further
understand the source of this excess.

experiments

the mass splitting region with  m(˜±

the ﬁrst time since the LEP2 experiment. Compared to the soft 2L search, which targets
the same event topology with high- pT hadronic jet, missing transverse momentum, and
two low-pT leptons from ˜0
2 ! ˜0
1`+` decay, this search has extended the search region

=== PDF PAGE 150 ===
down to smaller mass splitting regions by the combination of the two following e↵orts:
• Signiﬁcant suppression of low- pT fake lepton tracks by the development of low- pT
lepton track identiﬁcation methods,
• More sophisticated signal and background separation by a pNN-based event selec-
tion method.
The soft 2L search required two standard leptons or one standard lepton plus an
unidentiﬁed isolated track. The lower pT bound for standard electrons and muons were
4.5 GeV and 3 GeV, respectively, which are the same as the standard lepton criteria
used in this search. The event selection was performed by imposing uniform cuts on all
considered mass points.
On the other hand, this search requires one standard lepton and one low- pT lepton
identiﬁed track, with lower pT bounds reaching 0.5 GeV and 1 GeV for the electron chan-
nel and muon channel, respectively. The introduction of these algorithms contributes
signiﬁcantly to the suppression of low- pT fake lepton tracks, which was also the main
background of the one lepton plus one isolated track channel of the soft 2L search. Fur-
thermore, the event selection of this search is performed by a pNN, considering the event
topology dependencies on the mass splitting, as shown in Fig 6.4 and Fig. 6.5.C o m p a r e d
to the dominant W +jets background, the event topology di↵er more for smaller mass
splitting values, which has also contributed to the increased sensitivity in the correspond-
ing regions.
Comparison to the CMS experiment
The CMS experiment has also performed a higgsino search similar to the ATLAS soft
2L search [ 175]. Fig. 11.2 shows the exclusion limits set on simpliﬁed higgsino models
set by the CMS experiment. Higgsino mass of up to 150 GeV has been excluded for
m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) = 3 GeV, however, regions having smaller mass splittings have not been
considered. Therefore, as of today, this search sets the most stringent limit on simpliﬁed
higgsino models in the targeted mass regions of  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) = 2–3 GeV.

11.4 Impact on SUSY mass parameters
As described in Section 1.2.3,t h em a s s e so ft h en e u t r a l i n o sa r er e p r e s e n t e db yt h ef u n d a -

can further be interpreted to examine the impact on these fundamental parameters.
In typical models such as NUHM models, the mass hierarchy relation are likely to
satisfy |µ| <M 1 <M 2.I n s u c h c a s e s , t h e s l i g h t m i x t u r e o f t h e b i n o c o m p o n e n t w i t h t h e
dominant higgsino component could lead to a non-trivial mass splitting value between the
light mass eigenstates. Hence, in this interpretation, the impact of the wino component is
assumed to be negligible; the relation |µ|⌧ M1 ⌧ M2 is assumed to examine the impact
of the results on only the higgsino and bino mass parameters, µ and M1. Under such
assumptions, the higgsino and bino mass parameters are given by

11.5 Prospects in future experiments
The results presented in this dissertation uses the full LHC Run 2 dataset collected by
the ATLAS experiment, corresponding to 140 fb 1 of proton-proton collision data atps = 13 TeV. It is worthwhile to estimate the expected sensitivity of this search method
in future experiments, especially with the ongoing LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC starting in
2029, to devise future search strategies. In this study, the performances of the low- pT
electron and muon track IDs in future experiments are assumed to be the same as LHC
Run 2 for simplicity.
Expected sensitivities of this search in LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC have been evaluated
as expected exclusion contours and discovery reaches by scaling the number of signal
and background yields in the SRs. The scaling factors are comprised of two elements as
summarized in Table 11.1:t h e i n t e g r a t e d l u m i n o s i t y r a t i o a n d t h e c r o s s s e c t i o n r a t i o .
The signal cross sections have been estimated to rise by about 10(15)% by the transition
from ps =1 3T e Vt o ps =1 3 .6(14) TeV. The background cross sections have been
estimated using those of the dominant W +jets processes; they are expected to increase
by 5(8)% by the energy increase in Run 3 (HL-LHC). The ﬁnal signiﬁcance is calculated
by the scaled signal yields, background yields, and the background uncertainties using
Eq. ( 10.1). Z> 1.645, corresponding to 95% CL, is required for exclusion, and Z> 5i s
required for discovery.
Table 11.1: Summary of the scaling factors used for evaluating the expected sensitivities for future
experiments. The integrated luminosity factors are derived by simply taking the ratio over 140 fb  1.
The cross section ratios correspond to the expected increase by the increase of center-of-mass energy:ps = 13 .6 TeV for LHC Run 3 and ps = 14 TeV for HL-LHC. Note that the 400 fb  1 of LHC Run
3 includes the Run 2 data of 140 fb  1, thus the cross section factors have been applied only to the
corresponding 260 fb  1.
LHC Run 3 (400 fb  1) HL-LHC (3000 fb  1) HL-LHC (4000 fb  1)
Integrated luminosity 2.86 21.4 28.6
Signal cross section 1.10 1.15 1.15
Background cross section 1.05 1.08 1.08
Fig. 11.5 shows the expected exclusion and discovery reach of this search in LHC Run
3 and HL-LHC, evaluated by the above method. In the case in which the higgsino does
not exist in the corresponding search region, the exclusion limit is expected to reach up
to higgsino mass of 160 GeV and 170 GeV for LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC, respectively.
The expected discovery reaches for LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC both lie within the observed
exclusion of the search presented in this dissertation; even if higgsinos exist in this target
mass splitting region, they cannot be discovered by the ATLAS experiment with the same
strategy. This gives out a signiﬁcant message that further improvements in the analysis
method are necessary to discover higgsinos in future LHC experiments.
Possible future improvements
Some improvements are proposed below for possible amelioration of the sensitivity to
higgsinos in future experiments, using the low-momentum lepton signatures targeted in
143

=== PDF PAGE 155 ===
Figure 11.5: Expected exclusion and discovery reach of this search in LHC Run 3 and HL-LHC. The
expected discovery reaches lie within the observed exclusion limit presented in this dissertation.
this search. The sensitivity of this search largely depends on the identiﬁcation of the low-
momentum leptons emitted from the neutral higgsino decay, ˜ 0
2 ! ˜0
1`+`.T h e r e f o r e ,
the ﬁrst-order improvements are expected to be of those related to the performance of
low-pT lepton identiﬁcation:
• Improvement of the low- pT lepton track identiﬁcation,
• Improvement of the standard lepton identiﬁcation, with possible extension to lower
pT regime,
• Addition of the 0 `2T channel.
The ﬁrst item, the improvement of the low- pT lepton track identiﬁcation, is the most
straightforward way of ameliorating the sensitivity of this search. This could be realized by
adding further information as inputs to the low- pT lepton track identiﬁcation algorithms.
For example, the shap e information of the top o-clusters could b e added for b oth electron
and muon track identiﬁcation, to further discriminate lepton-like tracks and hadron-like
tracks.
The second item, the improvement of the standard lepton identiﬁcation is also expected
to have signiﬁcant impact on the search. The current pT threshold of 4.5 (3) GeV of the
standard electron (muon) identiﬁcation sets a stringent limit on this search, as signal
leptons tend to have lower pT for the target  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1) = 2–3 GeV region. However, as
the standard electron and muon identiﬁcation criteria have tighter requirements on the
calorimeter energy thresholds and muon spectrometer hits, respectively, this item may
be more dicult to achieve, at least in the ongoing LHC Run 3 due to similar detector
performances.
The third item, the addition of the 0 `2T channel, could follow after the success of the
ﬁrst item. The requirement of the one standard ID lepton of this search is imposed to
144

=== PDF PAGE 156 ===
achieve sucient background reduction. If this could be achieved with the low- pT lepton
track identiﬁcation algorithms, addition of a channel requiring no standardly identiﬁed
lepton and two low- pT lepton-like tracks, could result in further sensitivity.
In addition to the aforementioned items, improvement of the event selection method
and statistical combinations with the 2L search and the displaced track search could
also be considered. However, these improvements are expected to have only second-order
impacts on the sensitivity to higgsinos.

=== PDF PAGE 157 ===
12. Conclusion
The dissertation presents the search for higgsinos using 140 fb 1 of proton-proton collisions
at center-of-mass energy of ps = 13 TeV at the LHC, collected by the ATLAS detector
in 2015–2018. The search targets ﬁnal states with a high- pT hadronic jet, large Emiss
T ,
and two low- pT leptons, which is the expected event topology of higgsino pair production
events with the neutral higgsino decay ˜ 0
2 ! ˜0
1`+`.T h e s e n s i t i v i t y t o h i g g s i n o s h a s
been drastically improved by the development and application of new low- pT electron
and muon track identiﬁcation algorithms and the introduction of neural-network-based
event selection techniques which consider the kinematic dependencies on  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1). The
dominant 1L + fake lepton track background has been estimated by a data-driven method
to reduce MC modeling uncertainties on the fake lepton track. In all Signal Regions, no
signiﬁcant excess of data has been observed.
Exclusion limits on the target higgsino models have been set at 95% conﬁdence level.
In the context of the simpliﬁed higgsino model with  m(˜0
2, ˜0
1)=2  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1), higgsino
mass of up to 130 GeV has been excluded for target regions of  m(˜±
1 , ˜0
1) = 1–2 GeV.
The limits in terms of higgsino mass have been extended by 40 GeV, compared to the
results from the LEP2 experiment. This is the ﬁrst search at the LHC to probe such mass
splitting regions; this search has successfully contributed to covering the “higgsino gap”
with the LHC.
146

=== PDF PAGE 158 ===
13. Contributions to the Level-1 Muon
Trigger System in Run 3
The muon trigger system is crucial for probing new physics in the ATLAS experiment;
although the search presented in this dissertation does not directly use the muon trigger, it
is exploited in many of the eciency measurements of the objects used in this search, such
as the muon and the low- pT muon track in the muon channel. In addition to the search for
higgsinos, signiﬁcant e↵orts have been put into leading the development, commissioning,
and the operation of the TGC detector and Level-1 muon trigger systems for LHC Run
3. This section brieﬂy summarizes the contributions, primarily made during my Master’s
program in 2019–2021.
13.1 Run 3 upgrade of the Level-1 muon trigger
As described earlier in Section 2.2.7, the ATLAS Level-1 muon trigger is a hardware-
based system, which triggers physics events of interest by identifying muon candidates in
the RPC and TGC detectors for barrel and endcap regions, respectively. For LHC Run
3, the Level-1 endcap muon trigger has been signiﬁcantly upgraded from Run 2; New
Small Wheel (NSW) [ 177]d e t e c t o ra n do t h e ri n n e rm u o nd e t e c t o r sh a v eb e e ni n t r o d u c e d
to reduce fake muon trigger signals originating from protons emerging from the beam
pipe and toroid magnets, as illustrated in Fig. 13.1.B y t a k i n g t h e c o i n c i d e n c e b e t w e e n
the TGC and the inner muon detectors, reduction of fake muon trigger signals has been
achieved as shown in Fig. 13.2 [178].
Since the inputs to the trigger logic have largely increased due to the introduction
of new detectors, the backend trigger logic boards had to be newly designed. Therefore,
New Sector Logic (New SL) boards [ 179], capable of processing the endcap muon trigger
using the inputs from TGC and NSW detectors, have been developed. The surrounding
systems, which for example handle clock signal distribution, trigger readout, and trigger
output processing, have also been developed accordingly.
13.2 Realization of integrated control
In the summer of 2019, the development of the Run 3 Level-1 muon trigger was still at its
preparatory stage, where tests of the new electronics, including the New SL boards, were
still carried out at single board level. The implementation of the new electronics and their
corresponding software applications to the ATLAS control system was of utmost priority
147

the fake muon trigger signals.

scrutinization of the initialization procedures of each electronic device and the communica-
tion procedures of each optical link between the electronics. Furthermore, the robustness
of the system has been enhanced by the implementation of monitoring functionalities of
the new electronics, which are capable of detecting possible erros and hardware failures.
The softwares and functions described here are currently in use in the ongoing Run 3
data-taking.
13.3 Development of a new software-based readout
system
For o✏ine validation and p erformance evaluation of the Run 3 Level-1 endcap muon
trigger algorithm, the trigger logic input and output data must be read out. This was
originally done by a hardware-based system in Run 2, however, a new software-based
readout system called Software-based Readout Driver (SROD) [ 180], has been developed
for Run 3 to improve ﬂexibility and to cope with large increase in trigger input data.
Stable trigger readout has been achieved at 100 kHz by scrutinization and modiﬁcation
of the event-building software and evaluating its performance.
Fig. 13.3 shows an overview of the SROD and its software structure. The SROD is
designed to run on a commercially available high-performance servers. A single SROD
receives trigger inputs and outputs from 12 New SL boards via TCP/IP protocol. It
adopts a multi-process architecture to collect data and build the event data in parallel.
The “EventBuilder” application is responsible for formatting the ﬁnal event data, which is
sent out the ATLAS Readout System (ROS). Therefore, the EventBuilder application has
the heaviest load, and its performance determines the performance of the SROD system.
For stable operation at the nominal Level-1 trigger rate of 100 kHz, the event-building
must be processed within 10 µso na v e r a g e .
The processing speed of the EventBuilder application has been improved by countless
code optimizations including the reduction of certain variable types and the use of array
of function pointers. As a result, the performance of the SROD reached well beyond that
was expected to be required for Run 3 as shown in Fig. 13.4.T h e p e r f o r m a n c e s s h o w n
in these ﬁgures have been evaluated in 2021, however, further modiﬁcations have been
carried out before the start of Run 3 in 2022.
13.4 Commissioning and stable operation
Signiﬁcant contributions have been made not only by the developments of the softwares
and systems, but also by means of commissioning and stable operation. Contributions
to the commissioning include the one with ATLAS central trigger system immediately
before Run 3; the timing of the endcap muon trigger signals, crucial for data-taking,
has been successfully adjusted by analyzing signals from cosmic muon runs and initial

Figure 13.4: The performance of EventBuilder application in the SROD, as a function of data size and
trigger rate [ 180]. At the nominal trigger rate of 100 kHz, the SROD system can handle up to 4800 bits
per event, which is more than 2.5 times larger than the expected size of 1750 bits in Run 3. For a ﬁxed
data size of 1760 bits per event, the SROD system can run stably up to the speed of 170 kHz, which is
well beyond the nominal trigger rate.

proton-proton collision runs. Contributions to the stable operation of the system include,
• Handling unexpected errors and promptly recovering the system during physics
data-taking,
• Supporting team members to deal with such problems on-site at CERN and remotely
from Tokyo,
which have been done countless times in 2022–2023.
13.5 Summary
Through the developments and implementations of the new Level-1 muon trigger system,
its tests with surrounding systems and adjustment of signals have been realized in an op-
portune manner. Consequently, the Level-1 muon trigger system has successfully started
its operation at the start of LHC Run 3, realizing stable data-taking necessary for further
physics analysis.
